\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
be a graded perfect complex with
\mathcal{C}^{i}=0
for
i\geqslant 2
. Since
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
is perfect,
\mathcal{C}^{i}=0
for
i\ll 0
. Let
Q\in\Der_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))
be a degree one derivation that vanishes on the zero section, and satisfies
[Q,Q]=0
. Together,
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
and
Q
define a quasi-smooth stack over
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
, which we now describe.

Let
\bm{A}=(A,d_{A})
be a cdga concentrated in nonpositive degrees. For each
\operatorname{Spec}(\bm{A})
-point
f\colon\operatorname{Spec}(\bm{A})\rightarrow\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
, the derivation
f^{*}Q
is given by the data of
A
-linear degree one morphisms
m^{\vee}_{n}\colon f^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})^{\vee}\rightarrow\Sym_{\bm{A}}^{n}(f^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})^{\vee})
, which vanishes for
n\gg 0
. The vanishing at the origin means that
m^{\vee}_{0}=0
. The condition
[Q,Q]=0
implies
(m_{1}^{\vee})^{2}=0
and so
f^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})^{\vee}
inherits a differential
f^{*}d_{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq m_{1}^{\vee}
.
We define the dg algebra

\bm{B}=(\Sym_{\bm{A}}((f^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})^{\vee}),d_{A}+Q).

We define the (higher) stack
\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
over
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
, sending
(f\colon\operatorname{Spec}(\bm{A})\rightarrow\bm{\mathfrak{M}})
to
\operatorname{Spec}(\bm{B})
. Here the spectrum of a cdga (not necessarily negatively graded) is as defined and discussed in
[
77
,
10
]
. The functor
\operatorname{Spec}
from commutative differential graded algebras to derived stacks is the right adjoint to the derived global sections functor on derived stacks
[
10
, Proposition 3.1]
and if
\bm{B}
is concentrated in nonnegative degrees, then
\operatorname{Spec}(\bm{B})
is an ordinary higher stack. Note that although
\operatorname{Spec}(\bm{A})
is a derived affine scheme,
\operatorname{Spec}(\bm{B})
may not be, since if
\mathcal{C}^{i}\neq 0
for
i<0
then
\bm{B}
is not concentrated in nonpositive degrees. There is a natural quasi-isomorphism

(4.4)

\mathbb{L}_{\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})/\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}\simeq((f^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})^{\vee},f^{*}d_{\mathcal{C}}).

Definition 4.5
.

Assume that for all commutative algebras
A
, and all
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
-points
f\colon\operatorname{Spec}(A)\rightarrow\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
, the complex
(f^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet},f^{*}d_{\mathcal{C}})
has tor-amplitude
[-1,1]
. In particular, this implies that the projection
\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
is a quasi-smooth morphism of 1-Artin derived stacks. We say that the quasi-smooth morphism
\bm{\pi}\colon\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
is
globally presented
. A
global presentation
of a quasi-smooth morphism of 1-Artin stacks
\bm{f}\colon\bm{\mathfrak{N}}\rightarrow\bm{\mathfrak{N}}^{\prime}
is a commutative diagram of 1-Artin stacks in which the vertical arrows are equivalences of stacks

\textstyle{\bm{\mathfrak{N}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bm{f}}
\textstyle{\bm{\mathfrak{N}}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bm{\pi}}
\textstyle{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}.}

4.3.1.
Global equivalences

Definition 4.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet},Q_{\mathcal{C}})
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\bullet},Q_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}})
be global presentations of quasi-smooth morphisms, with both
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\bullet}
graded vector bundles over the same stack
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
. Let

\bm{f}\colon\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\bullet})

be a morphism over
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
such that
Q_{\mathcal{C}}=\bm{f}^{*}Q_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
, and such that the induced morphism of complexes
((\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})^{\vee},d_{\mathcal{C}})\rightarrow((\mathcal{C}^{\prime\bullet})^{\vee},d_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}})
is a quasi-isomorphism. We deduce from (
4.4
) that
\mathbb{L}_{\bm{f}}=0
. Thus
\bm{f}
is étale, with connected fibres, and so
\bm{f}
induces an equivalence of derived stacks, which we also denote

(4.5)

\displaystyle\bm{f}\colon\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{Q_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{Q_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\bullet}).

We call an equivalence defined via such an
\bm{f}
a
global equivalence
.

4.3.2.
Composition and pullback

Let
\bm{f}\colon\bm{\mathfrak{N}}\rightarrow\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
be a morphism of derived stacks, and assume that we are given a graded vector bundle
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
on
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
along with a derivation
Q\in\Der_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))
vanishing on the zero section. Then we obtain a Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{N}}}^{\bm{f}^{*}Q}(\bm{f}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{Q}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathfrak{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

In particular, globally presented quasi-smooth morphisms are stable under pullback.

A crucial feature of globally presented quasi-smooth morphisms of stacks is that they are closed under composition. We only need a very weak version of this claim. Let
\bm{\mathfrak{M}},\mathcal{C}^{\bullet},Q
be as above. Let
\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}
be a graded complex on
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
, which we pull back to a graded complex
\pi_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}
on
\bm{\mathfrak{T}}=\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
. Let
Q^{\prime}
be a degree one element of
\Der_{\bm{\mathfrak{T}}}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{T}}}(\pi_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}))
, vanishing on the zero section
\bm{\mathfrak{T}}
, and satisfying
[Q^{\prime},Q^{\prime}]=0
. Set
K
to be the image of
Q^{\prime}
under
\Der_{\bm{\mathfrak{T}}}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{T}}}(\pi_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}))\rightarrow\Der_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{T}}}(\pi_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}))
. The above equation, along with
[Q,Q]=0
, yields

\displaystyle[\pi_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{*}Q+K,\pi_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{*}Q+K]=0.

In particular, there is an equivalence

\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{T}}}^{K}(\pi_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\bullet})\simeq\operatorname{Tot}^{Q+K}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\bullet})

along with a natural projection

\pi_{\bm{\mathfrak{T}}}\colon\operatorname{Tot}^{Q+K}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{Q}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})

Definition 4.7
.

We call morphisms
\pi_{\bm{\mathfrak{T}}}
constructed as above
projections of globally presented quasi-smooth
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
-stacks
.

Definition 4.8
.

For a fixed stack
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
we define the category
\GPres_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}
to be the 2-category having as objects the global presentations
\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{Q}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
, 1-morphisms given by projections of globally presented quasi-smooth
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
-stacks (Definition
4.7
), and 2-morphisms defined by global equivalences as in §
4.3.1
.

We have shown that composition of 1-morphisms is well-defined, and it is obviously associative. We will not need any of the other compatibility conditions of this 2-category, and so we leave their verification to the interested reader.

4.3.3.
Total space of a perfect complex

We describe a special case of the above construction; in fact for most of the paper it is sufficient to understand this special case. Recall that a complex
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
on a classical stack
\mathfrak{M}
is called strictly
[-1,1]
-perfect if it can be represented by a complex of vector bundles

\mathcal{C}^{-1}\xrightarrow{d_{-1}}\mathcal{C}^{0}\xrightarrow{d_{0}}\mathcal{C}^{1}

in cohomological degrees
-1,0,1
. Given such a complex, the dual morphism
d^{\vee}
yields a degree 1 derivative
Q=d^{\vee}
on
\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
satisfying
[Q,Q]=0
since
d^{2}=0
, and so we may define the derived stack
\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
as above. If the derivative
Q=d^{\vee}
is induced as above from a differential on the complex
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
, we will often abuse notation and write

\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})\coloneqq\operatorname{Tot}^{d^{\vee}}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}).

We describe
t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))
explicitly, and show that it admits a description sheared of all mention of derived stacks. Let
A
be a commutative algebra, and let
f\colon\operatorname{Spec}(A)\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}
be an
A
-point of
\mathfrak{M}
, where we assume that the pullback
f^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
is trivialised. Then

\operatorname{Spec}(A)\times_{\mathfrak{M}}^{h}\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})\simeq\operatorname{Spec}((A[\bm{x}_{-1},\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{1}],Q))

where
\bm{x}_{n}=x_{n,1},\ldots x_{n,r_{n}}
is a set of generators in cohomological degree
n
,
r_{n}=\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{C}^{-n})
, and
Q
is the derivation determined by the dual
d^{\vee}
. We consider the homotopy coCartesian diagram of cdgas

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A[\bm{x}_{-1},\bm{x}_{0}],d^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A[\bm{y}_{0},\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{-1}],d^{\prime\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A[\bm{x}_{-1},\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{1}],Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A[\bm{y}_{0},\bm{x}_{-1},\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{1}],d^{\prime\prime\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}

The differentials are defined as follows:
d^{\prime}
is the differential induced by
Q
in the quotient ring
A[\bm{x}_{-1},\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{1}]/(\bm{x}_{1})
,
d^{\prime\prime\prime}
is the extension of
Q
defined by setting
d^{\prime\prime\prime}(\bm{y}_{0,j})=\bm{x}_{1,j}
and
d^{\prime\prime}
is the differential induced by
d^{\prime\prime\prime}
in the quotient ring
A[\bm{y}_{0},\bm{x}_{-1},\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{1}]/(\bm{x}_{1})
. Set
B=A[\bm{x}_{0}]/(Q\bm{x}_{-1})\simeq\tau^{\geqslant 0}((A[\bm{y}_{0},\bm{x}_{-1},\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{1}],d^{\prime\prime\prime}))
. Since
\operatorname{Spec}
and the classical truncation functor
t_{0}
preserve limits, we obtain a Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(B[\bm{y}_{0}])\cong\operatorname{Spec}(B)\times\mathbb{A}^{r_{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{0}(\operatorname{Spec}((A[\bm{x}_{-1},\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{1}],Q)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Spec}(B).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}

Here,
p
is the projection, and
a
is defined by the action of
\mathcal{C}^{-1}
on the total space
\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\ker(d_{0}))
. Globally, we find that
t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))
is the classical quotient stack of
\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\ker(d_{0}))
by the action of
\mathcal{C}^{-1}
.

The above analysis can be repeated with minor modification for the case of a longer complex of vector bundles

\mathcal{C}^{-n}\xrightarrow{d_{-n}}\mathcal{C}^{-n+1}\xrightarrow{d_{-n+1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{d_{0}}\mathcal{C}^{1}

under the assumption that
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
has tor-amplitude
[-1,1]
.

4.4.
Virtual pullbacks for globally presented morphisms

4.4.1.
Definition of virtual pullback

We begin with the definition of virtual pullback at the level of mixed Hodge module complexes, for which we assume that the underlying classical stack
\mathfrak{M}=t_{0}(\bm{\mathfrak{M}})
has an acyclic cover in the sense of Definition
3.3
. We assume that we are given a morphism
a\colon\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
to a quasiprojective scheme. Under our assumptions,
\mathfrak{E}\coloneqq t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{Q}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))
has an acyclic cover. We seek to define a virtual pullback morphism

a_{*}v_{\mathcal{C}}\colon a_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\rightarrow a_{*}(\pi_{\mathfrak{M}})_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{E}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})}.

Since
\mathfrak{M}
has an acyclic cover, we may pull back to a scheme and assume that
\mathfrak{M}
is itself a scheme, which we will label
X
. We define the category
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(X))
as in §
3.2
. Consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tot}_{Y}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\coloneqq t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{X}^{Q}(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\coloneqq t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{X}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

in which
V=\pi_{X}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
s
is the section induced by the sum of the morphisms of coherent sheaves

m_{i}^{\vee}\colon(\mathcal{C}^{1})^{\vee}\rightarrow\Sym_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}((\mathcal{C}^{0})^{\vee}).

The morphism
\pi_{X}
is the projection of an affine stack fibration, so that there is a natural isomorphism

l\colon\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{X}\rightarrow(\pi_{X})_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{Y}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})}.

We define the morphism
v_{s}\colon\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{Z}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{C}^{1})}

via refined Gysin pullback along
s
, and define the virtual pullback

(4.6)

a_{*}v_{\mathcal{C}}\colon a_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{X}\rightarrow c_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{Z}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})}

as the composition
(b_{*}v_{s})\circ(a_{*}l)
.

At the level of derived categories of constructible complexes, there is no need to assume that any of the above spaces are varieties, and setting
X=\mathcal{M}=\mathfrak{M}
we define the virtual pullback

k_{X}\rightarrow c_{*}k_{Z}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})]

as in (
4.6
).

4.4.2.
Base change

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
\bm{f}\colon\bm{\mathfrak{N}}\rightarrow\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
be a 1-representable morphism of derived stacks, let
\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{Q}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\bm{\mathfrak{M}}
be a globally presented quasi-smooth morphism, so we have the Cartesian diagram of stacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X=t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{N}}}^{f^{*}Q}(f^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y=t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}^{Q}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathfrak{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathfrak{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Set
h=\pi_{\mathfrak{M}}\circ g
.

(1)

Assume that
f
is proper. Then the following diagram (in which the horizontal morphisms are provided by adjunction) commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{X}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{\mathfrak{M}})_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{Y}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{\mathfrak{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}k_{\mathfrak{M}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\ast}v_{f^{\ast}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}}}

(2)

Assume that
f
is smooth of relative dimension
d
. Then the following diagram (in which the horizontal morphisms are provided by adjunction) commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{X}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{\mathfrak{M}})_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{Y}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})+2d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{\mathfrak{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}k_{\mathfrak{M}}[2d].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\ast}v_{f^{\ast}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}}}

If
\mathfrak{M}=t_{0}(\bm{\mathfrak{M}})
has an acyclic cover, the same statements are true at the level of mixed Hodge module complexes.

Proof.

Follows from Corollary
4.2
.
∎

4.4.3.
Virtual pullbacks done classically

Let
\mathcal{C}^{-1}\xrightarrow{d_{-1}}\mathcal{C}^{0}\xrightarrow{d_{0}}\mathcal{C}^{1}
be a three term complex of vector bundles on a classical stack
\mathfrak{M}
. As in §
4.3.3
, the differential induces a derivation
Q
on
\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
satisfying
[Q,Q]=0
and so we may define the stack
t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))
. As in §
4.3.3
the stack
\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})
is just the quotient of
\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{0})
by the action of
\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{Q}(\mathcal{C}^{-1})
. We have a diagram of stacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{0}).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

As
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are smooth representable morphisms of stacks, and moreover affine fibrations, we obtain isomorphisms

\displaystyle\alpha\colon

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}k_{\mathfrak{M}}\rightarrow(\pi_{1})_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{0})}[-2\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{C}^{0})]

\displaystyle\beta\colon

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}k_{\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})}\rightarrow(\pi_{2})_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{0})}[-2\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{C}^{-1})]

\displaystyle\gamma=(\pi_{\ast}\beta^{-1}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})])\circ\alpha\colon

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}k_{\mathfrak{M}}\rightarrow\pi_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})].

The differential
d_{0}\colon\mathcal{C}^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{1}
defines a section
s
of
\pi^{*}\mathcal{C}^{1}
. We define the refined Gysin morphism

\pi_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})]\rightarrow\pi_{*}(i_{s})_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})_{s}}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})]

as in §
4.1
. Composing with
\gamma
, we obtain the virtual pullback
v_{\mathcal{C}}
. As always, if
\mathfrak{M}
has an acyclic cover, and we are provided with a morphism
\mathfrak{X}\rightarrow X
to a scheme
X
, the same holds true at the level of direct image mixed hodge module complexes on
X
. In particular, this refined pullback is defined without any essential reference to derived algebraic geometry.

We have an isomorphism

t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))\cong\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})_{s}

and under this isomorphism the refined Gysin morphism we have just defined agrees with §
4.4.1
.

4.4.4.
Composing virtual pullbacks

The following will play a crucial role in the proof of associativity of the relative Hall algebra product of §
9.1
.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a classical stack, and let

\textstyle{\bm{\mathfrak{F}}=\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{T}}}^{K}(\pi_{\mathfrak{M}}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\bullet})\simeq\operatorname{Tot}^{Q+K}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\bm{\mathfrak{T}}}}
\textstyle{\bm{\mathfrak{T}}=\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{Q}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\mathfrak{M}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}}

be a composition in
\GPres_{\mathfrak{M}}
. Then there is an equality of morphisms of constructible complexes

(4.7)

(((\pi_{\mathfrak{M}})_{*}v_{\pi_{\mathfrak{M}}^{*}\mathcal{E}}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet})]))\circ v_{\mathcal{L}}=v_{\mathcal{L}\oplus\mathcal{E}}\colon\mathbb{D}k_{\mathfrak{M}}\rightarrow(\pi_{\mathfrak{M}}\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{T}})_{*}(\mathbb{D}k_{\mathfrak{F}})[-2\vrank(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\bullet})].

Assume moreover that
\mathfrak{M}
is an Artin stack with an acyclic cover, and that we have a commutative diagram of morphisms of stacks where
\mathcal{M}
is a scheme

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathfrak{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\mathfrak{T}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathfrak{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\mathfrak{M}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}.}

Then there is an equality of morphisms of complexes of mixed Hodge modules

\displaystyle\beta_{*}v_{\pi^{*}_{\mathfrak{M}}\mathcal{E}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{\vrank(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet})}\circ\gamma_{*}v_{\mathcal{L}}=\gamma_{*}v_{\mathcal{L}\oplus\mathcal{E}}\colon\gamma_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\rightarrow\alpha_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{F}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{\vrank(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\bullet})}.

In words, virtual pullback is functorial for global compositions.

Proof.

We prove the result in the category of constructible complexes, the mixed Hodge module proof is identical. Denote by

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\colon\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\leqslant 0})\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle\colon t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}^{Q+K}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\leqslant 0}))\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}

the natural projections, and set

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tot}_{\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\leqslant 0})}(a^{*}\mathcal{L}^{1})

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tot}_{t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}^{Q+K}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\leqslant 0}))}(d^{*}\mathcal{E}^{1}).

Consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\leqslant 0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A=\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\leqslant 0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B=t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C=\operatorname{Tot}^{Q+K}_{\mathfrak{M}}((\mathcal{L}\oplus\mathcal{E})^{\leqslant 0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D=t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}^{Q+K}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\leqslant 0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{Q+K}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}^{Q+K}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\leqslant 0}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{a^{*}\mathcal{L}^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{d^{*}\mathcal{E}^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\urcorner}

We denote by
a,b,c,d
the morphisms from
A,B,C,D
, respectively, to
\mathfrak{M}
. Then the central square of the above diagram yields the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{A}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{L}^{\leqslant 0})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{B}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{C}[-2\vrank((\mathcal{L}\oplus\mathcal{E})^{\leqslant 0}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{*}\mathbb{D}k_{D}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\leqslant 0})]}

in which all arrows are given by pullback or refined Gysin pullback. This diagram commutes by Proposition
4.9
. So the left hand side of the claimed equality in (
4.7
) is given by composing pullback along
a\pi
in the following diagram with refined Gysin pullbacks along
(a\pi)^{*}s
and
s^{\prime}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tot}_{C}((a\pi)^{*}\mathcal{L}^{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{Q+K}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a\pi)^{*}s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{(a\pi)^{*}\mathcal{L}^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{d^{*}\mathcal{E}^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}

The result then follows from Proposition
4.3
.
∎

4.4.5.
Functoriality under global equivalences

Proposition 4.11
.

Assume we are given the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bm{\mathfrak{N}}=\operatorname{Tot}^{Q_{\mathcal{C}}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bm{\mathfrak{N}}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Tot}^{Q_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{M}}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\bullet})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\pi^{\prime}}}

in which
\bm{\pi},\bm{\pi}^{\prime}
are globally presented quasi-smooth morphisms and
\bm{f}
is a global equivalence between them. As usual we write
\mathfrak{M}=t_{0}(\bm{\mathfrak{M}})
,
\mathfrak{N}=t_{0}(\bm{\mathfrak{N}})
,
\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}=t_{0}(\bm{\mathfrak{N}}^{\prime})
. Then the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\mathfrak{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}k_{\mathfrak{N}}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}_{\ast}k_{\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}}[-2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the vertical isomorphism is induced by the isomorphism
f=t_{0}(\bm{f})
. Assume, in addition, that
\mathfrak{M}
has an acyclic cover, and we are given a morphism
a\colon\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
to a scheme. Then the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{*}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{*}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{N}}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{*}\pi^{\prime}_{*}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}v_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}v_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Proof.

We prove the constructible complex version of the theorem, the mixed Hodge module version is proved in the same way. We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Tot}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\leqslant 0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Tot}(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Tot}(\pi^{\prime*}\mathcal{C}^{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Tot}(\pi^{*}\mathcal{C}^{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Tot}(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Tot}(\pi^{\prime*}\mathcal{C}^{\prime 1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Tot}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\leqslant 0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\leqslant 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime*}f^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{d^{\prime 1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{d^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\leqslant 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where all the squares are Cartesian (since
f
is a quasi-isomorphism), and we have left out some subscripts in order to reduce clutter. In particular,
Z=t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}^{Q_{\mathcal{C}}}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))\cong t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}^{Q_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\bullet}))
. The maps out of
Z
are the natural inclusions, the map
v
is the natural morphism induced by
f^{0}
coming from the identification
\operatorname{Tot}(\pi^{*}\mathcal{C}^{1})\cong\operatorname{Tot}((f^{\leqslant 0})^{*}\pi^{\prime*}\mathcal{C}^{1})
. The map
u
is the zero section of the vector bundle
\operatorname{Tot}(\pi^{\prime*}\mathcal{C}^{1})\rightarrow\operatorname{Tot}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\leqslant 0})
. The maps in this diagram satisfy the following additional properties:

(1)

The maps
f^{\leqslant 0}
and
v
are l.c.i. of codimension
\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\leqslant 0})-\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\leqslant 0})
,

(2)

The map
s_{d^{1}}
is l.c.i. of codimension
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{C}^{1})
,

(3)

The map
s_{d^{\prime 1}}
is l.c.i. of codimension
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime 1})
.

Since
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\bullet}
are quasi-isomorphic, we have in particular
\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})=\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\bullet})
, which means that
v\circ s_{d^{1}}
and
s_{d^{\prime 1}}
are both of the same codimension,
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime 1})
. The maps
f^{\leqslant 0}
and
v
are also of the same codimension and hence these Cartesian squares have no excess intersection bundle. Via Lemma
4.4
, we obtain that the virtual pullbacks by
\pi\circ q
and
\pi^{\prime}\circ(f^{\leqslant 0}\circ q)
coincide.
∎

4.4.6.
Derived equivalences

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a classical stack. Let
(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet},d_{\mathcal{C}})
and
(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet},d_{\mathcal{L}})
be perfect complexes in
\mathcal{D}(\Coh(\mathfrak{M}))
, with
\mathcal{C}^{i}=\mathcal{L}^{i}=0
for
i\geqslant 2
, and with tor-amplitude
[-1,1]
, and let us assume that there is an isomorphism between these two complexes in
\mathcal{D}(\Coh(\mathfrak{M}))
. We would like to show that these two complexes determine equal virtual pullbacks.

To start with, we make a weak technical assumption on the base stack
\mathfrak{M}
, namely that it has the
resolution property
. This is the condition that every coherent sheaf on
\mathfrak{M}
is the quotient of a vector bundle. In
[
78
]
it is proved that having the resolution property is equivalent to being a quotient of a quasi-affine scheme by a finite type affine group scheme. In particular, it is stable under taking Cartesian products.

We recall some basic homological algebra:

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
have the resolution property. Then every complex
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
of coherent sheaves that is perfect, considered as an object in the derived category of coherent sheaves on
\mathfrak{M}
, admits a quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
where
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
is a bounded complex of vector bundles with
\mathcal{L}^{i}=0
for
i>m(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})\coloneqq\max\{j\mid\mathcal{H}^{j}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})\neq 0\}
.

Proof.

Via the quasi-isomorphism
\tau^{\leqslant m}\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
we may assume that
\mathcal{F}^{i}=0
for
i>m\coloneqq m(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
. By the resolution property we can find a surjection
f_{m}\colon\mathcal{L}^{m}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{m}
from a vector bundle. This defines a morphism of complexes
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
, with
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
concentrated in degree
m
. Let
\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}
be the resulting double complex. Then
m(\mathcal{G}^{\bullet})<m(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
. Repeating the procedure for
\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}
, we define recursively maps

g_{l}=d_{l-1}\oplus f_{l}\colon\mathcal{L}^{l-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{l}\oplus\mathcal{F}^{l-1},

for
l\in[n,m]
where
n=\min\{-j\mid\exists\mathcal{G}\in\Coh(\mathfrak{M})\;\textrm{s.t.}\;\mathcal{E}\!xt^{j}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet},\mathcal{G})\neq 0\}
.
This lower bound exists because
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
is perfect, and hence isomorphic in
\mathcal{D}(\Coh(\mathfrak{M}))
to a bounded complex of locally free coherent sheaves. Then
\mathcal{E}\!xt^{1}(\ker(g_{n}),\mathcal{G})=0
for all coherent sheaves
\mathcal{G}
. Setting
\mathcal{L}^{n-1}=\ker(g_{n})
provides the required resolution.
∎

Then we have the following:

Proposition 4.13
.

For
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
as introduced at the start of §
4.4.6
, an isomorphism
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}\cong\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
in
\mathcal{D}(\Coh(\mathfrak{M}))
induces an equivalence of stacks
f\colon t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))\simeq t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}))

and the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\mathfrak{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{*}k_{t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}))}[2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{*}f_{*}k_{t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}))}[2\vrank(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes, with
p\colon t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}))\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}
the projection. If
\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
is a morphism to a scheme, the same statement remains true at the level of direct image mixed Hodge modules on
\mathcal{M}

Proof.

By the resolution property and Lemma
4.12
, we can reduce the claim to the weaker claim, in which we assume we are given a quasi-isomorphism of vector bundles
f^{\vee}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
. The first claim follows from applying
t_{0}
to the equivalence (
4.5
). The second claim is Proposition
4.11
.
∎

5.
Moduli stacks of objects in
2
-dimensional categories

In this section we introduce the setting and notation for categories and their moduli spaces. We explain in detail the assumptions we make on our categories and their moduli theory, in order to define cohomological Hall algebras, and then in the 2CY case, define BPS algebras.

5.1.
Geometric setup

5.1.1.
Categorical setup

We let
\mathscr{C}
be a
\mathbf{C}
-linear dg-category,
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}\subseteq\bm{\mathfrak{M}}_{\mathscr{C}}
a
1
-Artin open substack of the stack of objects of
\mathscr{C}
(in the sense of
[
79
]
) and let
\mathfrak{M}=t_{0}(\bm{\mathfrak{M}})
be the classical truncation. We assume that
\mathfrak{M}
parametrises objects of an admissible (in the sense of
[
2
, §1.2]
) finite length Abelian subcategory
\mathcal{A}
of the homotopy category
\HO^{0}(\mathscr{C})
. For
X=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
,
X
-points of
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}_{\mathscr{C}}
correspond to pseudo-perfect
\mathscr{C}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}A
-modules
N
, i.e. bimodules
N
that are perfect as
A
-modules. Given a pair of such modules we obtain the dg
A
-module
\operatorname{RHom}_{\mathscr{C}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}A}(N,N^{\prime})
, in this way defining the RHom complex on
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}_{\mathscr{C}}^{\times 2}
and thus, by restriction, on
\mathfrak{M}^{\times 2}
.

5.1.2.
Base monoid

We let
\mathscr{C}
,
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathcal{A}
be as in §
5.1.1
. We assume that we have a monoid object
(\mathcal{M},\oplus)
in the category of Artin stacks and a morphism
\varpi\colon\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Exact}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi\times\varpi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}

where
\mathfrak{Exact}
denotes the stack of short exact sequences in
\mathcal{A}
,
q
is the projection onto the last and first terms of a short exact sequence and
p
is the projection onto the middle term. In all the examples that we consider,
\mathcal{M}
will actually be a scheme. We sometimes say that
\mathcal{M}
satisfying the above condition
splits short exact sequences
. If
\mathcal{M}_{a}
is a connected component of
\mathcal{M}
, we define
\mathfrak{M}_{a}\coloneqq\varpi^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{a})
.

5.2.
Assumptions for the construction of the relative CoHA product

For the construction of the relative Hall algebra product, we need three assumptions: one for the construction of the pushforward along
p
, one for the construction of the virtual pullback by
q
and one rather technical assumption concerning iterated virtual pullbacks along forgetful maps from stacks of three-step filtrations.

The following assumption is required in the definition of pushforward by
p
.

Assumption 1
.

(Assumption for the construction of the pushforward by
p
). Let
\mathfrak{Exact}
be the stack of short exact sequences in
\mathcal{A}
. We let
p\colon\mathfrak{Exact}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}
be the morphism forgetting the extreme terms of the short exact sequence. We assume that
p
is a proper and representable morphism of stacks.

Next we turn to the virtual pullback along
q
.
Let
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
be the RHom complex on
\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{M}
, and set
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}[1]
. The stack
\bm{\mathfrak{E}}=\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
carries a universal bundle of (shifted) homomorphisms of objects in
\mathscr{C}
, so that we have a natural identification
t_{0}(\bm{\mathfrak{E}})=\mathfrak{Exact}
(see
[
51
, Proposition 2.3.4]
for the stack of coherent sheaves on surfaces, or §
6.1.4
), and a morphism
\bm{q}\colon\bm{\mathfrak{E}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}
given by sending the morphism
f\colon\rho\rightarrow\rho^{\prime}
to
\mathrm{cone}(f)[1]
. Then we have
q=t_{0}(\bm{q})
.

Assumption 2
.

We assume that
\mathfrak{M}
has the resolution property, i.e. every coherent sheaf on
\mathfrak{M}
is a quotient of a vector bundle. For all
\mathcal{M}_{a},\mathcal{M}_{b}\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M})
we assume that the morphism
\bm{q}_{a,b}\colon\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}_{a}\times\mathfrak{M}_{b}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{a,b})\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{a}\times\mathfrak{M}_{b}
is represented by a globally presented quasi-smooth morphism
\bm{q}^{\prime}_{a,b}
in the sense of Definition
4.5
.

Assumption
2
is all that is required to define the Hall algebra structure in §
9
. The resolution property assumption is made so that the Hall algebra product we define does not depend on the choice of global presentation
\bm{q}_{a,b}^{\prime}
of
\bm{q}_{a,b}
. A convenient consequence of this assumption is that by Totaro’s criterion
[
78
]
it guarantees that all stacks appearing have an acyclic cover, since they are global quotient stacks. We are then free to define direct images of mixed Hodge module complexes from these stacks following §
3.2.1
.

By §
4.3.3
, for the second part of the assumption to be met, it is sufficient for
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{a,b}
to be quasi-isomorphic to a three term complex of vector bundles, which can be shown directly in all cases we consider in this paper. Furthermore, under these conditions, as explained in §
4.4.3
, the virtual pullback along
t_{0}(\bm{q}_{a,b})
can be defined using only material in §
4.1
. As such, the appearance of derived algebraic geometry is thus far somewhat unnecessary.

Assumption 3
.

We set
\bm{\mathfrak{F}ilt}=\operatorname{Tot}_{\bm{\mathfrak{E}}\times\mathfrak{M}}((\bm{q}\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{M}})^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
. Then
t_{0}(\bm{\mathfrak{F}ilt})
may be identified with the stack of three step filtrations of objects in
\mathcal{A}
. Given a three step filtration
\rho_{\bullet}=(0\subseteq\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho_{2}\subseteq\rho_{3})
we define

\displaystyle\pi_{1,2}(\rho_{\bullet})

\displaystyle\coloneqq 0\rightarrow\rho_{1}\rightarrow\rho_{2}\rightarrow\rho_{2}/\rho_{1}\rightarrow 0

\displaystyle\pi_{2,3}(\rho_{\bullet})

\displaystyle\coloneqq 0\to\rho_{2}/\rho_{1}\rightarrow\rho_{3}/\rho_{1}\rightarrow\rho_{3}/\rho_{2}\rightarrow 0

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\rho_{\bullet})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\rho_{1}

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(\rho_{\bullet})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\rho_{2}/\rho_{1}

\displaystyle\pi_{3}(\rho^{\bullet})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\rho_{3}/\rho_{2}.

For
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in\pi_{0}(\mathfrak{M})
we define
\bm{\mathfrak{F}ilt}_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}}\subseteq\bm{\mathfrak{F}ilt}
to be the open and closed substack for which the underlying classical stack parametrises three step filtrations such that
\pi_{i}(\rho_{\bullet})\in\mathfrak{M}_{a_{i}}
. Similarly, we define
\bm{\mathfrak{E}}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\subseteq\bm{\mathfrak{E}}
to be the substack of short exact sequences
0\rightarrow\rho^{\prime}\rightarrow\rho\rightarrow\rho^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 0
with
\rho^{\prime}
and
\rho^{\prime\prime}
corresponding to points of
\mathfrak{M}_{a_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{a_{2}}
respectively. For
a,b,c\in\pi_{0}(\mathfrak{M})
we assume that the diagram of morphisms of derived stacks

(5.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathfrak{F}ilt}_{a,b,c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}_{a}\times\bm{\mathfrak{E}}_{b,c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathfrak{E}}_{a,b}\times\mathfrak{M}_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}_{a}\times\mathfrak{M}_{b}\times\mathfrak{M}_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\delta}}

which yields

(5.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Filt}}_{a,b,c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{a}\times\mathfrak{Exact}_{b,c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Exact}_{a,b}\times\mathfrak{M}_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{a}\times\mathfrak{M}_{b}\times\mathfrak{M}_{c}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha=\pi_{1,2}\times\pi_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta=\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma=q\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta=\operatorname{id}\times q}

after applying
t_{0}
is represented by a diagram

(5.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}_{a,b,c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{a,b,c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}_{a,b,c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\delta}^{\prime}_{a,b,c}}

in
\GPres_{\mathfrak{M}_{a}\times\mathfrak{M}_{b}\times\mathfrak{M}_{c}}
, and there is a quasi-equivalence of global presentations between
\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}_{a,b,c}
and the pullback of
\bm{q}^{\prime}_{a+b,c}
along
\bm{p}_{a,b}\times\operatorname{id}
and between
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{a,b,c}
and the pullback of
\bm{q}^{\prime}_{a,b+c}
along
\operatorname{id}\times\bm{p}_{b,c}
.

Remark 5.1
.

Some words are in order, regarding Assumptions
2
and
3
, for the reader that is not well-versed in the terminology of derived stacks. Firstly, quasi-smoothness of a morphism
\bm{f}
to a stack
\mathfrak{M}
is the requirement that
\mathbb{L}_{\bm{f}}
is perfect and lies in cohomological degrees
(-\infty,1]
. Since each of these relative cotangent complexes is isomorphic (up to a shift by one, and base change), to the RHom complex on
\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{M}
, this implies, in particular, that
\mathcal{A}
has projective dimension at most two. This is why we focus on categories of global dimension at most 2.

Secondly, we note that in all cases that we consider in this paper, on each connected component of
\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{M}
, the complex
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
is isomorphic in the derived category to a three term complex of vector bundles
\mathcal{L}^{-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{1}
.
In this situation, the description of the derived stack
\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet})
is very explicit, as is the description of the underlying classical stack
\mathfrak{E}=t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}}(\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}))
(see §
4.4.3
). In particular, the description of the virtual pullback along the projection
q\colon\mathfrak{E}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{M}
is most naturally described without any reference to derived algebraic geometry, as in §
4.4.3
.

Remark 5.2
.

The reason for working with a larger class of morphisms than projections like
q
, defined by three term complexes of vector bundles, is that the morphism
\mathfrak{Filt}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{\times 3}
can be hard to write this way, essentially because the Maurer–Cartan equation ceases to be linear when we consider filtrations of length three or above.
This becomes significant in exactly one place: proof of associativity of the Hall algebra product on
\mathscr{A}_{\varpi}
and
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\varpi}
.
If the reader just wishes to learn the definition of this product, and is intending to skip the proof of associativity or accept that it is “the standard argument”, they can largely ignore the intrusion of derived algebraic geometry into this paper.

5.3.
Assumptions for construction of the BPS algebra

For the construction of the BPS sheaf/MHM and the BPS algebra, we impose stronger restrictions on the stack
\mathfrak{M}
and the categories
\mathscr{C},\mathcal{A}
. The first two assumptions are geometric:

Assumption 4
.

The stack
\mathfrak{M}
has a good moduli space
\mathfrak{M}\xrightarrow{\mathtt{JH}}\mathcal{M}
in the sense of
[
1
]
, and
\mathcal{M}
is a scheme.

The scheme
\mathcal{M}
is endowed with a monoid structure given by the direct sum:

\displaystyle\oplus\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\longmapsto x\oplus y

obtained from the direct sum
\oplus\colon\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}
and universality of the good moduli space
\mathtt{JH}\colon\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
.

Assumption 5
.

We assume that the morphism
\oplus
is finite.

Given that the morphism
\oplus
is easily seen to be quasi-finite, this amounts to requesting that
\oplus
is a proper morphism.

The final assumption is categorical, and is the vital ingredient in
formality
and the local neighbourhood theorem (Theorem
5.3
): see
[
21
]
for details.

Assumption 6
.

Given a collection of points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\in\mathcal{M}
parametrising simple objects
\underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}
of
\mathcal{A}
, the full dg subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
containing
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
carries a right 2-Calabi–Yau (2CY) structure in the sense of
[
4
]
.

In most, but not all, examples the right 2CY structure can be uniformly derived (using results of
[
4
]
) from the presence of a
left
2CY structure on the category
\mathscr{C}
. For
\mathscr{C}
the dg-category of coherent sheaves on a smooth symplectic surface, a left 2CY structure is induced by a trivialisation of the canonical bundle
K_{S}
. If
\mathscr{C}
is the dg-category of perfect modules over a dga
A
, a left 2CY structure on
\mathscr{C}
is equivalent to a 2CY structure on
A
in the usual sense. We refer the reader to
[
4
]
for details and proofs.

In the rest of this section we recall some constructions and facts regarding the moduli spaces that appear under the above assumptions.

5.4.
Geometry of moduli spaces in the 2CY case

5.4.1.
Serre subcategories

Let
\mathfrak{M}\xrightarrow{\mathtt{JH}}\mathcal{M}
be as in Assumption
4
a good moduli space for the stack of objects
\mathfrak{M}
as in §
5.1.1
. Fix a locally closed submonoid
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
stable under direct summands (if
x\oplus y\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, then
x,y\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
). We define
\mathcal{B}
to be the full Abelian subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
generated by the objects represented by the closed points of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
under taking extensions. Typically,
\mathcal{B}
will be defined to be the full subcategory of objects of
\mathcal{A}
satisfying some support or nilpotency condition. See §
8.3
for examples crucial for this paper. Since
\mathcal{M}
parametrises semisimple objects in
\mathcal{A}
, we have that
\mathcal{B}
is a Serre subcategory.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a Serre subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
(an Abelian subcategory stable under extensions, subobjects and quotients) corresponding to a locally closed submonoid
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
. We define
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
to be the substack parametrising objects of
\mathcal{B}
, which we define via the following Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{JH}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

5.4.2.
Grading

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a fixed Serre subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
. Let
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})
be the monoid of connected components of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}
. The monoid structure
\oplus\colon\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})\times\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})\rightarrow\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})
is induced by the direct sum: for any
a,b\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})
, there exists a unique
c\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})
such that for any
(x,y)\in a\times b
,
x\oplus y\in c
. We set
a\oplus b=c
.

The monoid of connected components of the stack
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B}}
coincides with
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})
(since the fibers of
\mathtt{JH}
are connected, by
[
1
, Theorem 4.16 vii)]
). For
a\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})
, we denote by
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B},a}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B},a}
the corresponding connected components.

For any object
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathcal{B}
(resp. closed
\mathbf{C}
-point
x
of
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B}}
or
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}
), we let
[\mathcal{F}]
(resp.
[x]
) be the connected component of
x
. We will refer to
[x]
as
the class
of
x
.

By Assumption
2
, the restriction
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{a,b}
of the shifted RHom complex to
\mathfrak{M}_{a}\times\mathfrak{M}_{b}
can be represented by a bounded complex of vector bundles. Consequently, the Euler form
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})_{\mathscr{C}}=\sum_{i\in\mathbf{Z}}(-1)^{i}\operatorname{ext}^{1}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})=-\vrank(\mathcal{C}_{a,b}^{\bullet})
is constant for objects
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}
of
\mathcal{B}
, corresponding to points in fixed connected components
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B},a},\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B},b}
of
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{B}}
. Therefore, the Euler form factors through
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})
:

(-,-)_{\mathscr{C}}\colon\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})\times\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}})\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}.

5.4.3.
Geometric setup for the local neighbourhood theorem

We give the hypotheses we need to formulate Theorem
5.3
(see
[
21
]
). Let
\mathscr{C}
,
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathcal{M}
be as above satisfying Assumptions
4
,
5
and
6
. Then the closed points of
\mathcal{M}
are in bijection with semisimple objects of the category
\mathcal{A}
and the map
\mathtt{JH}
sends a
\mathbf{C}
-point of
\mathfrak{M}
corresponding to an object
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathcal{A}
to the
\mathbf{C}
-point of
\mathcal{M}
corresponding to the associated graded of
\mathcal{F}
with respect to some Jordan–Hölder filtration. Since
\mathcal{A}
is of finite length, each
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is a simple object of the category
\mathcal{A}
.

5.4.4.
\Sigma
-collections

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an object in a
\mathbf{C}
-linear Abelian category
\mathcal{A}
. We say that
\mathcal{F}
is a
\Sigma
-object
if for some
g\geqslant 0
,

\dim\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1\quad&\text{ if $i=0,2$}\\
&2g\quad&\text{ if $i=1$}\\
&0\quad&\text{ otherwise.}\end{aligned}\right.

Let
\underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}
be a collection of objects of
\mathcal{A}
. We say that
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is a
\Sigma
-collection
if each
\mathcal{F}_{t}
is a
\Sigma
-object and for any
m\neq n
,
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{m},\mathcal{F}_{n})=0
.

5.4.5.
The Ext-quiver of a
\Sigma
-collection

Let
\underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}
be a
\Sigma
-collection of objects of
\mathcal{A}
. The Ext-quiver of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is the quiver
\overline{Q}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
having as set of vertices
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
, and
\operatorname{ext}^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j})
arrows from
i
to
j
.

If
\operatorname{ext}^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j})=\operatorname{ext}^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{j},\mathcal{F}_{i})
for any
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant r
and this number is even if
i=j
, then
\overline{Q}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
is the double of some (non-unique) quiver
Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
. This condition is satisfied for right
2
-Calabi–Yau categories as in §
5.1.1
. We will refer to
Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
as
a half of

\overline{Q}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
, or as
a half of the Ext-quiver of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
.

We assume that we have a good moduli space and make Assumptions
4
,
5
and
6
. We have a morphism of monoids

\begin{split}\psi_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}&\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M})\\
\mathbf{m}&\longmapsto\sum_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}[\mathcal{F}_{i}]\end{split}

The pullback by
\psi_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
of the Euler form on
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M})
is the same as the pullback of the Euler form of
\Pi_{Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}
on
\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
. We also have a morphism of monoids

\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}

mapping
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
to the point of
\mathcal{M}
corresponding to the object
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}}
.

5.4.6.
The local neighbourhood theorem

We recall the local neighbourhood theorem for
2
-Calabi–Yau categories proved in
[
21
, Thm.5.11]
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
\mathtt{JH}\colon\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
be a good moduli space locally of finite type with reductive stabilizer groups such that
\mathfrak{M}=t_{0}(\bm{\mathfrak{M}})
is an Artin stack, where
\bm{\mathfrak{M}}\subseteq\bm{\mathfrak{M}}_{\mathscr{C}}
is an open substack of the stack of objects of a dg-category
\mathscr{C}
. Let
x
be a closed
\mathbf{C}
-valued point of
\mathfrak{M}
corresponding to an object
\mathcal{F}=\bigoplus_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}}
and assume that the full dg-subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
containing
\mathcal{F}_{i}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
carries a right 2CY structure and
\underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{i}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}
is a
\Sigma
-collection. We let
Q
be the half Ext-quiver of the collection
\underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r\}
. Then, there is a pointed affine
\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{m}}
-variety
(H,y)
, and an analytic neighbourhood
U\subseteq H
of
y
such that if
\mathcal{U}_{x}
(resp.
\mathfrak{U}_{x}
) denotes the image of
U
by the canonical map
H\rightarrow H/\!\!/\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{m}}
(resp.
H\rightarrow H/\mathrm{GL}_{\mathbf{m}}
), there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{m}}(\Pi_{Q}),0_{\mathbf{m}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathfrak{U}_{x},y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathfrak{M},x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{m}}(\Pi_{Q}),0_{\mathbf{m}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{U}_{x},p(y))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{M},\mathtt{JH}(x))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{JH}_{\mathbf{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{JH}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\jmath}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath_{0_{\mathbf{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\jmath}_{0_{\mathbf{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

in which the horizontal morphisms are analytic open immersions and the squares are Cartesian.

The original version of this theorem gives an étale local description of the map
(\mathfrak{M},x)\rightarrow(\mathcal{M},\mathtt{JH}(x))
but for our purposes, it is most convenient to work with the analytic topology.
Under the assumptions given in §
5.4.3
, the theorem applies for any
\mathbf{C}
-valued closed point of
\mathfrak{M}
.

5.4.7.
Geometry of the good moduli space

In this section, we deduce some geometric information regarding the good moduli space of the stack of objects of a
2
-Calabi–Yau category using the local neighbourhood theorem and
[
11
]
.

For
a\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})
, we define
p(a)=2-(a,a)_{\mathcal{A}}
. We let
R_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}=\{a\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})\mid p(a)\geqslant 0\}
and
\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}
be the set of non-zero
a\in R_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}
such that for any decomposition
a=\sum_{j=1}^{s}a_{j}
with
a_{j}\in R_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}
we have
p(a)>\sum_{j=1}^{s}p(a_{j})
. Finally, we let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}^{s}
be the open locus of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}
over which
\mathtt{JH}_{a}
is a
\mathbf{C}^{*}
-gerbe. It parametrises simple objects of
\mathcal{A}
of class
a
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
a\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})
. We have the following properties:

(1)

The set
\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}
is the set of
a\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})
for which
\mathtt{JH}_{a}
is generically a
\mathbf{C}^{*}
-gerbe,

(2)

If
a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}
, then
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}
is irreducible of dimension
p(a)
and its smooth locus is
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}^{s}
,

(3)

If
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is a
\Sigma
-collection in
\mathcal{A}
and
Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
a half of the Ext-quiver of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
, then
\psi_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{-1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}})=\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}}
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward, combining Theorem
5.3
and
[
11
, Theorem 1.2]
together with the compatibility of Euler forms with
\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
, defined in §
5.4.5
.
∎

6.
2-dimensional categories from geometry and algebra

The categories conforming to the geometric setup of §
5.1
and §
5.2
that we consider in this paper come from two constructions, and are of geometric or algebraic origin. In this section we explain these two constructions, and check that the various assumptions of §
5.1
and §
5.2
are met.

6.1.
Geometry

Let
X
be a complex projective variety. To start with we do not make any assumption on the smoothness or dimension of
X
.

6.1.1.
The moduli stack of semistable coherent sheaves

We fix an embedding
X\rightarrow\mathbf{P}^{N}
of
X
in some projective space, so that we have a distinguished choice of a very ample line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{X}(1)
on
X
obtained as the restriction to
X
of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{P}^{N}}(1)
. For
\mathcal{F}
a coherent sheaf on
X
we write
\mathcal{F}(n)\coloneqq\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}(1)^{\otimes n}
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a coherent sheaf on
X
. We say that
\mathcal{F}
is strongly generated by
\mathcal{O}_{X}(n)
if the natural map

\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(n),\mathcal{F})\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\mathcal{O}_{X}(n)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}

is an epimorphism of coherent sheaves and

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(n),\mathcal{F})=\HO^{i}(X,\mathcal{F}(-n))=0

for
i>0
.

Let
P(t)\in\mathbf{Q}[t]
be a polynomial and
\mathfrak{Coh}_{n,P(t)}(X)
be the substack of
\mathfrak{Coh}_{P(t)}(X)
parametrising coherent sheaves on
X
with Hilbert polynomial
P(t)
, strongly generated by
\mathcal{O}_{X}(-n)
. As the conditions of being an epimorphism and of vanishing of cohomology are open conditions, this is an open substack of
\mathfrak{Coh}_{P(t)}(X)
. It can be realised as the global quotient of an open subscheme
Q_{X}(n,P(t))
of the Quot-scheme
\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t)):=\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(-n)\otimes\mathbf{C}^{P(n)},P(t))
of quotients
\mathcal{O}_{X}(-n)^{P(n)}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathcal{F}
where
\mathcal{F}
has Hilbert polynomial
P(t)
. The open subscheme

Q_{X}(n,P(t))\subseteq\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t))

consists of epimorphisms
\mathcal{O}_{X}(-n)\otimes\mathbf{C}^{P(n)}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathcal{F}
such that
\mathcal{F}
is strongly generated by
\mathcal{O}_{X}(-n)
and
\varphi
induces an isomorphism
\mathbf{C}^{P(n)}\rightarrow\HO^{0}(X,\mathcal{F}(n))
.

There is a natural action of
\mathrm{GL}_{P(n)}
on
Q_{X}(n,P(t))
, via the action on the factor
\mathbf{C}^{P(n)}
of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(-n)\otimes\mathbf{C}^{P(n)}
. We have

\mathfrak{Coh}_{n,P(t)}(X)\simeq Q_{X}(n,P(t))/\mathrm{GL}_{P(n)}.

It is known (see
[
34
, Sec.2]
) that the
\mathrm{GL}_{P(n)}
action on
\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t))
admits a linearisation, from which it follows that the
\mathrm{GL}_{P(n)}
-quotient
\mathfrak{Coh}_{n,P(t)}(X)
satisfies the resolution property.

6.1.2.
Projectivity

Let
P(t)
and
R(t)
be polynomials. We let
\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t),R(t))
be the Quot-scheme parametrising quotients
\mathcal{O}_{X}(-n)^{P(n)}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathcal{F}\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathcal{G}
where
\mathcal{F}
(resp.
\mathcal{G}
) has Hilbert polynomial
P(t)
(resp.
R(t)
). We let
Q_{X}(n,P(t),R(t))
be the open subscheme of such quotients for which
\mathcal{F}
is strongly generated by
\mathcal{O}_{X}(-n)
and
\varphi
induces an isomorphism
\mathbf{C}^{P(n)}\rightarrow\HO^{0}(X,\mathcal{F}(n))
. The action of
\mathrm{GL}_{P(n)}
on
\mathcal{O}_{X}(-n)\otimes\mathbf{C}^{P(n)}
induces an action of
\mathrm{GL}_{P(n)}
on
Q_{X}(n,P(t),R(t))
. Then,

Q_{X}(n,P(t),R(t))/\mathrm{GL}_{P(n)}\simeq\mathfrak{Exact}_{n,P(t),R(t)}(X),

where
\mathfrak{Exact}_{n,P(t),R(t)}(X)
is the stack of short exact sequences
0\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}\rightarrow 0
where
\mathcal{H}
is strongly generated by
\mathcal{O}_{X}(-n)
,
\mathcal{H}
has Hilbert polynomial
P(t)
and
\mathcal{G}
has Hilbert polynomial
R(t)
. We have a map
\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t),R(t))\rightarrow\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t))
forgetting the map
\psi
, and a similar map obtained by restriction
\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t),R(t))\rightarrow\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,R(t))
.

The map
\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t),R(t))\rightarrow\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t))
is projective as both
\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t),R(t))
and
\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t))
are projective schemes over
\mathbf{C}
.

By definition, we have a Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q_{X}(n,P(t),R(t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q_{X}(n,P(t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t),R(t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Quot}_{X}(n,P(t))}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

By base change, the morphism
Q_{X}(n,P(t),R(t))\rightarrow Q_{X}(n,P(t))
is projective.

Proposition 6.1
.

The morphism of stacks

\displaystyle p_{P(t),R(t)}\colon\mathfrak{Exact}_{n,P(t),R(t)}(X)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathfrak{Coh}_{n,P(t)}(X)

\displaystyle(0\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}\rightarrow 0)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{H}

is a representable projective morphism of stacks. In particular,
p_{P(t),R(t)}
satisfies Assumption
1
from §
5.1
.

Proof.

This morphism is obtained from the quotient by
\mathrm{GL}_{P(n)}
of the natural projective morphism
Q_{X}(n,P(t),R(t))\rightarrow Q_{X}(n,P(t))
between schemes, so it is representable and proper.
∎

We define the set of
reduced polynomials
as equivalence classes, by imposing the equivalence relation on
\mathbf{Q}[t]
:
P(t)\sim Q(t)
if
P(t)=\lambda Q(t)
for some
\lambda\in\mathbf{Q}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. We denote equivalence classes by lowercase letters
p(t)\in\mathbf{Q}[t]/\sim
. For
p(t)\in\mathbf{Q}[t]/\sim
we denote by
\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{p(t)}(X)
the stack of coherent sheaves which are either the zero sheaf, or semistable with reduced Hilbert polynomial
p(t)
. We denote by
\mathfrak{Exact}^{\sst}_{p(t)}(X)
the stack of short exact sequences of semistable coherent sheaves with reduced Hilbert polynomial
p(t)
.

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
p(t)\in\mathbf{Q}[t]/\sim
be a reduced polynomial. The morphism of stacks

\mathfrak{Exact}^{\sst}_{p(t)}(X)\rightarrow\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{p(t)}(X)

mapping a short exact sequence of Gieseker semistable coherent sheaves on
X
to the middle term is representable and projective, i.e.
\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{p(t)}(X)
satisfies Assumption
1
from §
5.1
.

Proof.

Fix a polynomial
P(t)
in the equivalence class
p(t)
. Since the leading term of any
Q(t)
for which
\mathfrak{Coh}_{Q(t)}(X)
is nonempty is a positive integer divided by
\deg(p(t))!
, there are finitely may
R(t)\sim P(t)
for which
p_{P(t),R(t)}^{-1}(\mathfrak{Coh}_{n,P(t)}(X))
is nonempty.
If
j\colon\mathcal{G}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}
is a monomorphism of sheaves having the same reduced Hilbert polynomial where
\mathcal{F}
is semistable, then
\mathcal{G}
and
\operatorname{coker}(j)
are semistable. By boundedness of the moduli stack
[
34
]
, for sufficiently large
n

\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{P(t)}(X)\subseteq\mathfrak{Coh}_{n,P(t)}(X).

Projectivity of
\mathfrak{Exact}^{\sst}_{p(t)}(S)\rightarrow\mathfrak{Coh}_{p(t)}^{\sst}(X)
then follows from Proposition
6.1
by base change.
∎

6.1.3.
Resolving RHom

Fix a reduced Hilbert polynomial
p(t)
. So far we have placed few constraints on the projective variety
X
. In order for the stack
\mathfrak{M}=\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{p(t)}(X)
to satisfy all of the assumptions detailed in §
5.1
, we next assume that
S=X
has dimension at most 2 and is smooth.

Fix a polynomial
P(t)
in the equivalence class
p(t)
. We denote by
\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
the substack of coherent sheaves with Hilbert polynomial
P(t)
. Let
\mathcal{U}\in\Coh(\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\times S)
be the universal coherent sheaf. By boundedness of the moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}
we can pick
n_{1}\gg 0
such that
\mathcal{U}_{y}
is strongly generated by
\mathcal{O}_{S}(-n_{1})
for every sheaf
\mathcal{U}_{y}
corresponding to a point
y
of
\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}
. Let

\mathcal{L}^{1}=\mathcal{H}om_{\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\times S}(\pi_{2}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{S}(-{n_{1}}),\mathcal{U})\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{S}(-{n_{1}}),

and define
\mathcal{K}
to be the kernel of the adjunction morphism
\mathcal{L}^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
. By boundedness again, there is a
n_{2}\gg n_{1}
such that all sheaves
\mathcal{K}_{y}
parametrised by points
y
of
\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}
are strongly generated by
\mathcal{O}_{S}(-n_{2})
and we define
\mathcal{L}^{2}=\mathcal{H}om_{\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\times S}(\pi_{2}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{S}(-{n_{2}}),\mathcal{K})\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{S}(-{n_{2}})
. Let
\mathcal{L}^{3}
be the kernel of the adjunction morphism
\mathcal{L}^{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{1}
. The following is well-known

Lemma 6.3
.

The coherent sheaf
\mathcal{L}^{3}
is a flat family of vector bundles on
S
.

Proof.

Let
y
be a
K
-point of
\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}
for some field
K\supset\mathbf{C}
and let
x
be a point of
S_{K}
. Then
\operatorname{Ext}_{S_{K}}^{i}(\mathcal{L}_{y}^{3},\mathcal{O}_{x})\cong\operatorname{Ext}_{S_{K}}^{i+2}(\mathcal{U}_{y},\mathcal{O}_{x})=0
if
i\geqslant 1
, and the result follows.
∎

We have thus defined a global resolution
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
of the universal sheaf by vector bundles. Since the resolution depends on numbers
n_{2}\gg n_{1}\gg 0
we will denote this resolution by
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
when the dependence on these numbers is significant. Fix
R(t)\sim P(t)
. We abuse notation and also denote by
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
the resolution of the universal sheaf on
\mathfrak{M}_{R(t)}\times S
. For the next lemma we use boundedness again:

Lemma 6.4
.

Pick numbers
n^{\prime}_{2}\gg n^{\prime}_{1}\gg n_{2}\gg n_{1}\gg 0
. Let
y,y^{\prime}
be
K
-points of
\mathfrak{M}_{R(t)}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}
respectively for a field
K\supset\mathbf{C}
. Then
\operatorname{Ext}_{S_{K}}^{n}((\mathcal{L}^{i}_{n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}})_{y^{\prime}},(\mathcal{L}^{j}_{n_{1},n_{2}})_{y})=0
for
n\geqslant 1
and all
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
.

The lemma implies that the dimension of
((\pi_{1,2})_{*}\mathcal{H}om(\pi_{1,3}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{i}_{n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}},\pi_{2,3}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{j}_{n_{1},n_{2}}))_{y^{\prime\prime}}
is constant across all
K
-points
y^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathfrak{M}_{P(T)}\times\mathfrak{M}_{R(t)}
, from which we deduce the following

Corollary 6.5
.

Pick numbers
n^{\prime}_{2}\gg n^{\prime}_{1}\gg n_{2}\gg n_{1}\gg 0
, and
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
. For numbers
a\neq b
let
\pi_{a,b}
denote the projection of
\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\times\mathfrak{M}_{R(t)}\times S
onto its
a
th and
b
th factors. The coherent sheaf

\mathcal{V}^{i,j}_{(n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}),(n_{1},n_{2})}\coloneqq(\pi_{1,2})_{*}\mathcal{H}om(\pi_{1,3}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{i}_{n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}},\pi_{2,3}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{j}_{n_{1},n_{2}})

is a vector bundle on
\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\times\mathfrak{M}_{R(t)}
.

Note that
\mathcal{V}^{\bullet,\bullet}_{(n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}),(n_{1},n_{2})}
is a double complex, with differentials induced by the differentials on
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}}
. We denote by
\mathcal{R}^{\bullet}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),(n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2})}
the resulting total complex.

Corollary 6.6
.

The complex
\mathcal{R}^{\bullet}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),(n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2})}
provides a five term complex of vector bundles resolving the RHom complex on
\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\times\mathfrak{M}_{R(t)}
.

6.1.4.
Stacks of extensions

Set
\mathfrak{X}=t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\times\mathfrak{M}_{R(t)}}(\mathcal{R}^{\bullet}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),(n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2})}))\times S
. We denote by
\pi^{\prime}_{a,b}
the projection of
\mathfrak{X}
onto the
a
th and
b
th factors of
\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\times\mathfrak{M}_{R(t)}\times S
. By construction,
\mathfrak{X}
carries a universal cone double complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime*}_{2,3}\mathcal{L}^{3}_{n_{1},n_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime*}_{1,3}\mathcal{L}^{3}_{n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime*}_{2,3}\mathcal{L}^{2}_{n_{1},n_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime*}_{1,3}\mathcal{L}^{2}_{n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime*}_{2,3}\mathcal{L}^{1}_{n_{1},n_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime*}_{1,3}\mathcal{L}^{1}_{n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}}}

The first column is the pullback of the universal resolution
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}}
along the projection to
\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\times S
, the second column is the pullback of the universal resolution
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
along the projection to
\mathfrak{M}_{R(t)}\times S
, and the horizontal morphisms are determined by the fibre of the projection to
\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\times\mathfrak{M}_{R(t)}\times S
. We denote the total complex by
\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}
. Then since
\mathcal{E}^{i}=0

for
i>0
there is a morphism of complexes

\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{E}^{\bullet})

which is moreover a quasi-isomorphism, defining a morphism
q^{\prime}\colon\mathfrak{X}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{a+b}
and a tautological morphism

(6.1)

T\colon\mathcal{E}^{\bullet}\rightarrow q^{\prime*}\mathcal{U}.

We obtain the standard

Proposition 6.7
.

There is an equivalence of stacks

t_{0}(\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\times\mathfrak{M}_{R(t)}}(\mathcal{R}^{\bullet}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),(n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2})}))\simeq\mathfrak{Exact}^{sst}_{R(t),P(t)}(S).

Remark 6.8
.

The complex
\mathcal{R}^{\bullet}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),(n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2})}
is situated in cohomological degrees
[-2,2]
. There is a more economical presentation of the RHom complex, that will suffice for defining the relative CoHA product. We define

\mathcal{R}^{i}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),-}\coloneqq(\pi_{1,2})_{*}\mathcal{H}om(\pi_{1,3}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{i}_{n_{1},n_{2}},\pi_{2,3}^{*}\mathcal{U}),

For
n_{2}\gg n_{1}\gg 0
this defines a complex of vector bundles, in cohomological degrees
[0,2]
, and for
n_{2}\gg n_{1}\gg n^{\prime}_{2}\gg n^{\prime}_{1}\gg 0
the quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{L}^{j}_{n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
induces a quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{R}^{\bullet}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),(n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2})}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}^{\bullet}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),-}
.

In particular we obtain the following (well-known) proposition:

Proposition 6.9
.

Fix two non-zero polynomials
P(t),R(t)
with the same reduction (i.e. differing by scalar multiplication). Fix numbers
n_{2}\gg n_{1}\gg 0
. Then the RHom complex on
\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{P(t)}(S)\times\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{R(t)}(S)
is resolved by the three term complex of vector bundles
\mathcal{R}^{\bullet}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),-}
. In particular, Assumption
2
is satisfied by the morphism
\bm{q}\colon\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}_{P(t)}\times\mathfrak{M}_{R(t)}}(\mathcal{R}^{\bullet}_{(n_{1},n_{2}),-})\rightarrow\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{P(t)}(S)\times\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{R(t)}(S)
.

6.1.5.
Three-step filtrations

Fix non-zero polynomials
P_{m}(t)
, with
m=1,2,3
, such that all three polynomials have the same reduction. We denote
\mathfrak{M}_{i}=\mathfrak{Coh}_{P_{m}(t)}^{\sst}(S)
. We fix
n^{(1)}_{2}\gg n^{(1)}_{1}\gg n^{(2)}_{2}\gg n^{(2)}_{1}\gg n^{(3)}_{2}\gg n^{(3)}_{1}\gg 0
and define vector bundle complexes

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{i,j}_{a,b}\coloneqq(\pi_{1,2,3})_{*}\mathcal{H}om(\pi_{a,4}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{i}_{n^{(a)}_{1},n^{(a)}_{2}},\pi_{b,4}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{j}_{n^{(b)}_{1},n^{(b)}_{2}})[1]

on
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\times\mathfrak{M}_{2}\times\mathfrak{M}_{3}\times S
for each pair
a,b\in\{1,2,3\}
with
a<b
. We denote by
(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{a,b},d_{a,b})
the total complexes associated to these double complexes.
We set

\mathcal{C}_{1,2,3}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{C}_{1,2}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{2,3}^{\bullet}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{1,3}^{\bullet}

and denote by
b_{1}
the differential on
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,2,3}
. By construction, there is a morphism

b_{2}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{2,3}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,2}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{1,3}^{\bullet}

given by composition of morphisms. We define
Q\in\Der_{\mathfrak{M}_{a}\times\mathfrak{M}_{b}\times\mathfrak{M}_{c}}((\mathcal{C}_{1,2,3}^{\bullet})^{\vee})
by setting its linear part to be given by the dual of
b_{1}
, its quadratic part to be the dual of
b_{2}
, and setting all higher Taylor coefficients to be zero. Then we set
\bm{\mathfrak{F}ilt}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}_{a}\times\mathfrak{M}_{b}\times\mathfrak{M}_{c}}^{Q}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,2,3})
. The following is proved exactly the same way as Proposition
6.7
.

Proposition 6.10
.

There is an equivalence of stacks
\bm{\mathfrak{F}ilt}^{\prime}\simeq\bm{\mathfrak{F}ilt}_{P_{1}(t),P_{2}(t),P_{3}(t)}
, where
\bm{\mathfrak{F}ilt}_{P_{1}(t),P_{2}(t),P_{3}(t)}
is the stack of three term filtrations
0=\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{3}
of semistable coherent sheaves on
S
, with the Hilbert polynomials of
\mathcal{F}_{i}/\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
equal to
P_{i}(t)
for
i=1,2,3
respectively.

The diagram (
5.1
) is represented by the diagram in
\GPres_{\mathfrak{M}_{a}\times\mathfrak{M}_{b}\times\mathfrak{M}_{c}}
(Definition
4.8
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,2,3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{2,3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}_{1}\times\mathfrak{M}_{2}\times\mathfrak{M}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\alpha}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\beta}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\delta}^{\prime}}

where we have left out some instances of the subscript
\mathfrak{M}_{1}\times\mathfrak{M}_{2}\times\mathfrak{M}_{3}
to reduce clutter. This yields the first part of Assumption
3
. Over the base
\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}\times\mathfrak{M}_{2}\times\mathfrak{M}_{3}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,2})
the sum
b_{1}^{\vee}+b_{2}^{\vee}
is linear, i.e.
\bm{\beta}^{\prime}
is the global presentation associated to the complex of vector bundles
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{2,3}\oplus\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,3}
with differential
b_{1}+g
where
g\colon\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{2,3}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{1,3}^{\bullet}\otimes(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,2})^{\vee}
is induced by
b_{2}
. Explicitly, there is an isomorphism of complexes

\bm{\delta}^{\prime*}\left(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{2,3}\oplus\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,3}\right)\cong(\pi^{\prime}_{1,2,3})_{*}\mathcal{H}om(\pi^{\prime*}_{1,2,4}\mathcal{E}^{\bullet},\pi_{3,4}^{\prime*}\mathcal{L}^{\bullet})

where

\displaystyle\pi^{\prime}_{1,2,4}\colon

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}\times\mathfrak{M}_{2}\times\mathfrak{M}_{3}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,2})\times S\rightarrow\operatorname{Tot}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}\times\mathfrak{M}_{2}}(\mathcal{R}^{\bullet})\times S

\displaystyle\pi^{\prime}_{1,2,3}\colon

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}\times\mathfrak{M}_{2}\times\mathfrak{M}_{3}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,2})\times S\rightarrow\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}\times\mathfrak{M}_{2}\times\mathfrak{M}_{3}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,2})

\displaystyle\pi^{\prime}_{3,4}\colon

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tot}^{Q}_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}\times\mathfrak{M}_{2}\times\mathfrak{M}_{3}}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{1,2})\times S\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{3}\times S

are the natural projections. We denote by
