\mathfrak{F}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{M}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}
defined in (
11
). The section of this vector bundle induced by
\mu
is the section
s_{\mu}
described in (
11
).

9.2.3.
The comparison diagram

We have the following commutative diagram with Cartesian squares.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)}}\times\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)}}\times\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathfrak{M}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{F}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathfrak{M}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathfrak{Z}}_{\overline{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\overline{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}_{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This diagram (without the left-most map) comes from the quotient by
P_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}
of the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)}}\times X_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{F}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{F}_{\overline{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}\times\mathfrak{n}_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{F}_{\overline{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{Z}_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{\overline{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)}}\times i_{\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}\times 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}_{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The outer square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)}}\times X_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{\overline{Q},\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

is the left-most Cartesian square of (
8.1
) (before quotienting by
P_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}
) used to defined the refined pullback in the relative version of the cohomological Hall algebra product of Schiffmann–Vasserot. The top-right square together with the top-left arrow is the diagram used to define the relative version of the cohomological Hall algebra in §(
9.1
).

The map
q^{\prime}_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}
is smooth, so in particular l.c.i. and so that the left-most square has no excess intersection bundle (
q^{\prime}_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}
and
q_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}
are both of codimension
-\sum_{\char 16\relax\xrightarrow{\alpha}j\in\overline{Q}_{1}}(\mathbf{d}^{(1)})_{i}(\mathbf{d}^{(2)})_{j}
). The morphisms
s_{\mu}
,
s^{\prime}_{\mu}
are also l.c.i. (as sections of vector bundles) of the same codimension (that equals
\dim\mathfrak{n}_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}
). By Lemma
4.4
we obtain the identification of the two Hall algebra products constructed in §
8.2
and §
9
, yielding the following theorem:

Theorem 9.7
.

For any
\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}
, the morphisms
m_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}
defined in §
8.2
and
m_{\mathbf{d}^{(1)},\mathbf{d}^{(2)}}
defined in §
9.1
(for the stack of representations of a preprojective algebra) coincide.

9.3.
The cohomological algebra of a
\Sigma
-collection

Let
\underline{x}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
be pairwise distinct closed
\mathbf{C}
-points of
\mathcal{M}
represented by a set
\underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}
of simple objects of
\mathcal{A}
, where the full subcategory containing
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
carries a right 2CY structure, and thus these objects form a
\Sigma
-collection. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\underline{x}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}
be the submonoid generated by
\underline{x}
. We have a Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{x}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\underline{x}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{JH}_{\Pi_{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\underline{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{JH}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\underline{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where
\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{x}}
parametrises
\Pi_{Q}
-modules whose simple subquotients are in
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
.

The cohomological Hall algebra of
\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{x}}
is defined to be
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\underline{x}}:=(\mathtt{JH}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\underline{x}})_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{x}}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{-\chi/2}\cong i_{\underline{x}}^{!}(\mathtt{JH}_{\Pi_{Q}})_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}}
, with the multiplication
i_{\underline{x}}^{!}m
.

Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1})
be a quiver and let
\Pi_{Q}
be its preprojective algebra. We let
\mathrm{Nil}
be the
\Sigma
-collection of one-dimensional nilpotent
\Pi_{Q}
-modules, so that
\underline{x}=\{S_{i}\mid i\in Q_{0}\}
. The cohomological Hall algebra of
\mathrm{Nil}
is called
the fully nilpotent cohomological Hall algebra
of
\Pi_{Q}
.

We now identify the cohomological Hall algebra of a
\Sigma
-collection.

Lemma 9.8

(
[
21
, Proof of Theorem 5.11]
)
.

Let
\underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}
be a
\Sigma
-collection in
\mathscr{C}
, and assume that the full subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
containing
\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}
carries a right 2CY structure. Let
Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
be a half of the Ext-quiver of
\mathcal{\underline{F}}
. Then the full dg-subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
generated under extensions by
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
is quasi-equivalent to the full dg-subcategory of
\mathcal{D}^{\bdd}_{\dg}(\mathrm{mod}^{\mathscr{G}_{2}(Q_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}})})
generated under extensions by nilpotent one-dimensional modules.

Corollary 9.9
.

Let
\underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}
be a
\Sigma
-collection in
\mathcal{A}
, and assume that the full subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
containing
\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}
carries a right 2CY structure. Let
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
be the closed substack of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\mathcal{A}}
parametrising objects whose simple subquotients are in
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
. Let
Q
be a half of the Ext-quiver of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
. Let
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{nil}}
be the closed substack of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\Pi_{Q}}
parametrising nilpotent representations of
\Pi_{Q}
. We have an isomorphism
\Phi_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{nil}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
such that if
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
is the restriction of the shifted RHom complex over
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{A}}
to
\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\times\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{nil},\bullet}
is the restriction of the RHom complex over
\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}\times\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}
to
\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{nil}}\times\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{nil}}
, then
\Phi_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{nil},\bullet}
.

Corollary 9.10
.

The isomorphism of stacks
\Phi_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{nil}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
induces an isomorphism of cohomological Hall algebras

\Phi_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{*}\colon\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{nil}}.

Proof.

This is a consequence of Corollary
9.9
, since the cohomological Hall algebra of the
\Sigma
-collection
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
only depends on the stack
\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
together with the (shifted) RHom-complex
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
over
\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\times\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
up to quasi-isomorphism, by Propositions
4.11
and
4.13
.
∎

10.
BPS algebras

We introduce the BPS algebra of a 2-Calabi–Yau category. The BPS algebra is a smaller, more manageable, algebra than the cohomological Hall algebra. The general expectation from cohomological DT theory is that the cohomological Hall algebra should be (half) of a Yangian-type algebra associated to a Lie algebra, while what we define as the BPS algebra will be the universal enveloping algebra of this Lie algebra. We recall important facts about BPS algebras.

10.1.
The BPS algebra of a
2
-Calabi–Yau category

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathtt{JH}\colon\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
be as in §
5.3
. Recall that this means that as well as Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
, we require that there be a good moduli space
\mathtt{JH}\colon\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
, that the direct sum morphism
\oplus\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
is finite, and a 2CY condition on categories generated by simple objects (Assumptions
4
,
5
and
6
).

The relative cohomological Hall algebra is defined in §
8
as an algebra structure on the complex of mixed Hodge modules
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}}\coloneqq\mathtt{JH}_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}))
. As in the introduction (§
1.2.5
), we also make a choice of bilinear form
\psi
on
\mathbf{Z}^{\pi_{0}(\mathfrak{M})}
, and define
\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\psi}_{\mathtt{JH}}
to be the same underlying object as
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}}
but with the
\psi
-twisted multiplication

(10.1)

m^{\psi}_{a,b}=(-1)^{\psi(a,b)}m_{a,b}.

We write
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}}^{(\psi)}
in statements to indicate that the statement is true whether we include the
\psi
-twist or not. This
\psi
-twist is crucial for Theorems
10.7
and
1.5
.

Lemma 10.1
.

The mixed Hodge module complex
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}}^{(\psi)}
is concentrated in nonnegative degrees:
\mathcal{H}^{i}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{(\psi)}_{\mathtt{JH}})=0
for
i<0
.

Proof.

This is part of
[
21
, Theorem B]
. The statement is independent of whether we give
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}}
the
\psi
-twisted multiplication or not.
∎

Corollary 10.2
.

The map
\mathcal{H}^{0}(m^{(\psi)})\colon\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}})\boxdot\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}})
yields an algebra in the tensor category
(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}),\boxdot)
.

Proof.

This comes from the fact that
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}}
is concentrated in nonnegative degrees (Lemma
10.1
) and for any two mixed Hodge modules
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{M}
,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}))}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}[-n])=0
if
n>0
.
∎

Definition 10.3
.

We define
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathrm{Alg}}^{(\psi)}
to be the mixed Hodge module
\mathcal{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{(\psi)}_{\mathtt{JH}})
with the multiplication
\mathcal{H}^{0}(m^{(\psi)})
. We denote by
\mathcal{BPS}^{(\psi)}_{\mathrm{Alg}}={{}^{\mathfrak{p}}\!\mathcal{H}^{0}}(\mathscr{A}^{(\psi)}_{\mathtt{JH}})
the underlying perverse sheaf of
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathrm{Alg}}
, along with its induced
\boxdot
-algebra structure
\rat(\mathcal{H}^{0}(m^{(\psi)}))
. By abuse of terminology we call both
the BPS algebra sheaf
. Its cohomology is denoted
\aBPS^{(\psi)}_{\mathrm{Alg}}:=\mathbf{H}(\mathcal{BPS}^{(\psi)}_{\mathrm{Alg}})
and called
the BPS algebra
.

Lemma 10.4
.

The mixed Hodge module
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}^{(\psi)}_{\mathrm{Alg}}
is semisimple.

Proof.

Again, the statement is independent of whether we consider the
\psi
-twisted multiplication or not. This is a consequence of the decomposition theorem for 2-Calabi–Yau categories
[
21
, Theorem B]
.
∎

10.2.
Primitive summands

Lemma 10.5

(
[
21
]
)
.

Let
a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}
be a class such that
\mathcal{A}
has simple objects of class
a
.
Then, we have a canonical monomorphism of mixed Hodge modules

\phi_{a}\colon\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{a})\hookrightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathrm{Alg}}.

It induces a morphism of complexes of mixed Hodge modules via the natural
\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}}
-action on the target

\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{a})\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}}.

Proof.

Let
a
be as in the lemma. The scheme
\mathcal{M}_{a}
is irreducible with smooth locus
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{s}
, the locus of simple objects. Over this locus,
\mathtt{JH}
is a
\mathbf{C}^{*}
-gerbe. Therefore,
(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}})_{|\mathcal{M}_{a}^{s}}\cong\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{s})\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}}
. Since
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}}
has a
\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}}
-action coming from the determinant bundle on
\mathfrak{M}_{a}
, we obtain the second statement.
∎

By the universal property of free algebras in monoidal categories, the morphisms
\phi_{a}\colon\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{a})\hookrightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathrm{Alg}}
induce
\boxdot
-algebra morphisms

(10.2)

\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\psi)}\colon\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Alg}}\left(\bigoplus_{a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{a})\right)\longrightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathrm{Alg}}^{(\psi)}.

Lemma 10.6
.

The summands
\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{a})\subseteq\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}^{(\psi)}_{\mathrm{Alg}}
for
a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}
are primitive subobjects of
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}^{(\psi)}_{\mathrm{Alg}}
as a
\boxdot
-algebra,
i.e. if
\mathcal{I}_{a}
is the image by
\mathcal{H}^{0}(m^{(\psi)})
of
\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}b+c=a\\
b,c\neq 0\end{subarray}}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}^{(\psi)}_{\mathrm{Alg},b}\boxtimes\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}^{(\psi)}_{\mathrm{Alg},c}
, the composition
\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{a})\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}^{(\psi)}_{\mathrm{Alg},a}/\mathcal{I}_{a}
is non-zero.

Proof.

This follows by the proof of
[
21
, Theorem 7.35]
, and is immediate by support considerations: the supports of all simple direct summands of
\mathcal{I}_{a}
are included in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}\smallsetminus\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a}^{s}
.
∎

10.3.
Borcherds–Bozec algebra of a quiver

Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1})
be a quiver. Following Bozec’s definition in
[
6
]
we consider a Borcherds Lie algebra associated to this datum generalising the Kac–Moody Lie algebra of a loop-free quiver.

We decompose the set of vertices
Q_{0}=Q_{0}^{\real}\sqcup Q_{0}^{\iso}\sqcup Q_{0}^{\hyp}
where the set of
real
vertices
Q_{0}^{\real}
is the set of vertices carrying no loops, the set of
isotropic
vertices
Q_{0}^{\iso}
is the set of vertices carrying exactly one loop and the set of
hyperbolic
vertices
Q_{0}^{\hyp}
is the set of vertices carrying at least two loops. The set of isotropic and hyperbolic vertices
Q_{0}^{\imag}=Q_{0}^{\iso}\sqcup Q_{0}^{\hyp}
is the set of
imaginary
vertices.

The set of simple roots of the Borcherds–Bozec algebra of
Q
,
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}
, is

I_{\infty}=(Q_{0}^{\real}\times\{1\})\sqcup(Q_{0}^{\imag}\times\mathbf{Z}_{>0}).

There is a natural projection

\displaystyle p\colon\mathbf{Z}^{(I_{\infty})}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}^{Q_{0}}

\displaystyle(f\colon I_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathbf{Z})

\displaystyle\longmapsto\left(pf\colon i^{\prime}\mapsto\sum_{(i^{\prime},n)\in I_{\infty}}nf(i^{\prime},n)\right).

The lattice
\mathbf{Z}^{Q_{0}}
has a bilinear form given by the symmetrized Euler form,
(-,-)
. We endow
\mathbf{Z}^{(I_{\infty})}
with the bilinear form
p^{*}(-,-)
obtained by pulling-back the Euler form, and by abuse of notation we also denote this form on
\mathbf{Z}^{(I_{\infty})}
by
(-,-)
. In explicit terms,

(1_{(i^{\prime},n)},1_{(j^{\prime},m)})=mn(1_{i^{\prime}},1_{j^{\prime}})=mn(\langle 1_{i^{\prime}},1_{j^{\prime}}\rangle_{Q}+\langle 1_{j^{\prime}},1_{i^{\prime}}\rangle_{Q}).

There is a Borcherds algebra associated to the data
(\mathbf{Z}^{(I_{\infty})},p^{*}(-,-))
. It is the Lie algebra over
\mathbf{Q}
with generators
h_{i^{\prime}},e_{i},f_{i}
, with
i^{\prime}\in Q_{0}
,
i\in I_{\infty}
, satisfying the following set of relations.

(10.3)

\displaystyle{[h_{i^{\prime}},h_{j^{\prime}}]}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ for $i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in Q_{0}$}

\displaystyle[h_{j^{\prime}},e_{(i^{\prime},n)}]

\displaystyle=n(1_{j^{\prime}},1_{i^{\prime}})e_{(i^{\prime},n)}

\displaystyle\text{ for $j^{\prime}\in Q_{0}$ and $(i^{\prime},n)\in I_{\infty}$}

\displaystyle[h_{j^{\prime}},f_{(i^{\prime},n)}]

\displaystyle=-n(1_{j^{\prime}},1_{i^{\prime}})f_{(i^{\prime},n)}

\displaystyle\text{ for $j^{\prime}\in Q_{0}$ and $(i^{\prime},n)\in I_{\infty}$}

\displaystyle\operatorname{ad}(e_{j})^{1-(j,i)}(e_{i})=\operatorname{ad}(f_{j})^{1-(j,i)}(f_{i})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ for $j\in Q_{0}^{\real}\times\{1\}$, $i\neq j$}

\displaystyle[e_{i},e_{j}]=[f_{i},f_{j}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ if $(i,j)=0$}

\displaystyle[e_{i},f_{j}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{i,j}nh_{i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\text{ for $i=(i^{\prime},n)$.}

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}
has a triangular decomposition

\mathfrak{g}_{Q}=\mathfrak{n}_{Q}^{-}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{Q}^{+}

where
\mathfrak{n}_{Q}^{-}
(resp.
\mathfrak{n}_{Q}^{+}
, resp.
\mathfrak{h}
) is the Lie subalgebra generated by
e_{i},i\in I_{\infty}
(resp.
f_{i},i\in I_{\infty}
, resp.
h_{i^{\prime}},i^{\prime}\in Q_{0}
) and we will only be interested in its positive part
\mathfrak{n}_{Q}^{+}
. It is generated by
e_{i},i\in I_{\infty}
with Serre relations

\displaystyle\operatorname{ad}(e_{j})^{1-(j,i)}(e_{i})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ for $j\in Q_{0}^{\real}\times\{1\}$, $i\neq j$}

\displaystyle[e_{i},e_{j}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ if $(i,j)=0$}.

If
Q
is a totally negative quiver, then it has no real vertex and
(i,j)<0
for every
i,j\in I_{\infty}
, so
I_{\infty}
is the
free
Lie algebra generated by
e_{i},i\in I_{\infty}
.

By considering the
associative
algebra generated by
e_{i},f_{i}
,
i\in I_{\infty}
and
h_{i^{\prime}}
,
i^{\prime}\in Q_{0}
subject to the relations (
10.3
), one obtains an algebra
\UEA(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})
.
It is the enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}
and it admits a triangular decomposition

\UEA(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})=\UEA(\mathfrak{n}_{Q}^{-})\otimes\UEA(\mathfrak{h})\otimes\UEA(\mathfrak{n}_{Q}^{+}).

10.4.
The degree
0
BPS algebra of the strictly seminilpotent stack

10.4.1.
The cohomological Hall algebra of the strictly seminilpotent stack

The absolute cohomological Hall algebra of the category of strictly seminilpotent representations of
Q
is

\HO^{*}\!\!\!\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SSN}}:=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}}\HO_{*}^{\BoMo}(\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{SSN}},\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}),

and has been defined in §
8.3.2
. We denote by
\HO^{*}\!\!\!\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi,\mathcal{SSN}}
the same algebra, with the multiplication twisted by the sign
(-1)^{\psi(-,-)}
, for some choice of bilinear form
\psi
satisfying
\psi(a,b)+\psi(b,a)=(a,b)_{\Pi_{Q}}
modulo 2. For concreteness the reader may like to choose
\psi(a,b)=\langle a,b\rangle_{Q}
.

The seminilpotent stack
\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{SSN}}
is a Lagrangian substack of
\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
. Therefore, its irreducible components are of dimension
-\langle\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}\rangle
. By
[
7
, Theorem 1.15]
, for
\mathbf{d}=1_{i^{\prime}}
, where
i^{\prime}\in Q_{0}
is a vertex with
g_{i^{\prime}}
loops, the irreducible components of
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{SSN}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
are parametrised by

(1)

\{\star\}
if
g_{i^{\prime}}=0
,

(2)

The set of partitions
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{r})
of
n
if
g_{i^{\prime}}=1
,

(3)

The set of tuples
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{r})
with
n_{j}>0
and
\sum_{j}n_{j}=n
if
g_{i^{\prime}}>1
.

In the cases (
2
) and (
3
), the partition/tuple can be described explicitly. Let
I_{r}\subseteq\Pi_{Q}
be the two-sided ideal generated by paths in
\overline{Q}
containing at least
r
arrows of
Q_{1}^{*}
. Let
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d},c}^{\mathcal{SSN}}
be an irreducible component of
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{SSN}}
and
\rho
a
\Pi_{Q}
–module corresponding to a general point in
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d},c}^{\mathcal{SSN}}
. Let
r
be the smallest integer such that
I_{r}\rho=0
. We have a sequence of inclusions

0=I_{r}\rho\subseteq I_{r-1}\rho\subseteq\ldots\subseteq I_{0}\rho=\rho.

Then,
n_{i}=\dim I_{i-1}\rho/I_{i}\rho
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
.

We let
\HO^{0}\!\!\!\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SSN}}
be the
degree zero strictly seminilpotent cohomological Hall algebra
.
It has a combinatorial basis given by fundamental classes of irreducible components of
\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SSN}}
,
[\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{SSN}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d},c}]
. This algebra is identified with the enveloping algebra of the Borcherds–Bozec Lie algebra. This is proven in detail in
[
32
, Theorem 1.3]
:

Theorem 10.7
.

There is an isomorphism of algebras

\UEA(\mathfrak{n}_{Q}^{+})\rightarrow\HO^{0}\!\!\!\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi,\mathcal{SSN}}

sending
e_{(i^{\prime},n)}
to
[\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{SSN}}_{n1_{i^{\prime}},(n)}]
.

Let
i\colon\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathcal{SSN}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}
be the natural inclusion.

Lemma 10.8
.

The natural map
{{}^{\mathfrak{p}}\!\tau^{\leqslant 0}}\mathscr{A}^{(\psi)}_{\Pi_{Q}}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{(\psi)}_{\Pi_{Q}}
induces a map
i^{!}{{}^{\mathfrak{p}}\!\tau^{\leqslant 0}}\mathscr{A}^{(\psi)}_{\Pi_{Q}}\rightarrow i^{!}\mathscr{A}^{(\psi)}_{\Pi_{Q}}=:\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{(\psi),\mathcal{SSN}}
which induces an isomorphism

\mathbf{H}^{0}(i^{!}{{}^{\mathfrak{p}}\!\tau^{\leqslant 0}}\mathscr{A}^{(\psi)}_{\Pi_{Q}})\rightarrow\mathbf{H}^{0}(i^{!}\mathscr{A}^{(\psi)}_{\Pi_{Q}}).

Proof.

This appears in
[
19
, Section 6.4]
. Again, the proof is independent of whether we include the sign twist
\psi
or not.
∎

Corollary 10.9
.

Let
Q
be a totally negative quiver. The morphism

\HO^{0}(i^{!}\Phi^{(\psi)}_{\Pi_{Q}})\colon\HO^{0}\!\left(i^{!}\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Alg}}\left(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}}\mathcal{IC}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}})\right)\right)\rightarrow\HO^{0}\!\left(\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{(\psi),\mathcal{SSN}}\right)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We consider the
\psi
-twisted version of the statement: then the untwisted version will follow from Lemma
10.10
. The functor
i^{!}
is strict monoidal, so it commutes with the operator
\operatorname{Free}
. By §
10
, the subobject
\mathcal{IC}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}})\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{Q}}\mathcal{IC}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}})\rightarrow\mathcal{BPS}^{\psi}_{\mathrm{Alg}}={{}^{\mathfrak{p}}\!\tau^{\leqslant 0}}\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q}}
admits a direct sum complement
\mathcal{BPS^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{Alg}}
such that the multiplication map
m\colon\mathcal{BPS}^{\psi}_{\mathrm{Alg},a}\boxtimes\mathcal{BPS}^{\psi}_{\mathrm{Alg},b}\rightarrow\mathcal{BPS}^{\psi}_{\mathrm{Alg},a+b}
for
a,b\neq 0
factors through the inclusion
\mathcal{BPS}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{BPS}^{\psi}_{\mathrm{Alg}}
. Consequently, the multiplication map
\HO^{0}(i^{!}m^{\psi})\colon\HO^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi,\mathcal{SSN}})\otimes\HO^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi,\mathcal{SSN}})\rightarrow\HO^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi,\mathcal{SSN}})
factors through
\HO^{0}(i^{!}\mathcal{BPS}^{\prime})
.

If
\mathbf{d}=ne_{i^{\prime}}
for some
i^{\prime}\in Q_{0}
,
n\geqslant 1
, then,
\HO^{0}(i^{!}\mathcal{IC}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},ne_{i^{\prime}}}))=\mathbf{Q}e_{i^{\prime},n}
is one-dimensional (
[
21
, §6.4.4]
). By Theorem
10.7
, if
\mathbf{d}\neq ne_{i^{\prime}}
for
i^{\prime}\in Q_{0}
,
n\geqslant 1
, then
\HO^{0}(i^{!}\mathcal{BPS}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{d}})=\HO^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}^{\psi,\mathcal{SSN}})
. For such
\mathbf{d}
,
\HO^{0}(i^{!}\mathcal{IC}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}))
is a direct summand of
\HO^{0}(\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}^{\psi,\mathcal{SSN}})
with complement
\HO^{0}(i^{!}\mathcal{BPS}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{d}})
. It therefore vanishes. The LHS in the corollary is then equal to
\HO^{0}\!\left(\operatorname{Free}_{\mathrm{Alg}}\left(\bigoplus_{i\in I_{\infty}}\mathbf{Q}e_{i}\right)\right)
. By Theorem
10.7
again,
\HO^{0}(i^{!}\Phi^{\psi}_{\Pi_{Q}})
is an isomorphism.
∎

We finish this section by collecting some elementary lemmas regarding
\psi
-twisted algebras.

Lemma 10.10
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a
L
-graded algebra, where
L
is some Abelian group. Let
\psi
be a bilinear form on
L
, and define
\mathcal{B}^{\psi}
via
m_{a,b}^{\psi}=(-1)^{\psi(a,b)}m_{a,b}
, where
m_{a,b}
is the restriction of the multiplication in
\mathcal{B}
to the
a
th and
b
th graded pieces, and
m_{a,b}^{\psi}
is the same restriction, for
\mathcal{B}^{\psi}
. If
\mathcal{B}
is the free algebra generated by the graded subspace
V\subseteq\mathcal{B}
, then
\mathcal{B}^{\psi}
is freely generated by the same subspace
V\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\psi}
.

Proof.

Let
S
be a homogeneous basis for
V
. Given a (possibly empty) word
w
in the letters
S
, we write
m^{(\psi)}(w)
for the evaluation of the products on
w
, setting this to be the unit if
w
is empty. Then
m(w)=\pm m^{\psi}(w)
. Let
W
be the set of words in
S
. Then
\{m(w)\;\lvert\;w\in W\}
is a basis for
\mathcal{B}
if and only if
\{m^{\psi}(w)\;\lvert\;w\in W\}
is.
∎

Lemma 10.11
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a
L
-graded algebra, where
L
is some Abelian group. Let
\psi,\psi^{\prime}
be bilinear forms on
L
such that

\displaystyle\psi(a,b)+\psi(b,a)=\psi^{\prime}(a,b)+\psi^{\prime}(b,a)

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{mod}\;2.

Let
V\subseteq\mathcal{B}
be a
L
-graded subspace of
\mathcal{B}
. We consider
\mathcal{B}
as a Lie algebra in two different ways: firstly for the commutator Lie bracket
[-,-]_{\psi}
defined by the
\psi
-twisted multiplication, secondly for
[-,-]_{\psi^{\prime}}
defined with respect to the
\psi^{\prime}
-twisted multiplication. Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the Lie subalgebra generated by
V
under the first Lie bracket, let
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
be the Lie algebra generated under the second. Then as vector subspaces of
\mathcal{B}
, we have
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

If
\alpha
and
\beta
are homogeneous, the conditions imply that
[\alpha,\beta]_{\psi}=\pm[\alpha,\beta]_{\psi^{\prime}}
.
∎

11.
Some examples

The framework of §
9
concerns very general 2-dimensional categories satisfying the assumptions of §
5.2
. In subsequent sections, we first restrict ourselves to categories with a left 2-Calabi–Yau structure satisfying the additional assumptions of §
5.3
in order to define the BPS algebra of §
10
, and then restrict further to totally negative 2-Calabi–Yau categories, in order to prove our main results. Before making these restrictions, we discuss general examples of 2-dimensional categories that the above Hall algebra construction applies to, as well as example applications of our main theorems for totally negative 2CY categories.

11.1.
Degree zero sheaves on surfaces

We use our constructions to generalise a PBW result concerning the CoHA of zero-dimensional support coherent sheaves on a smooth surface
S
from
[
51
]
to mixed Hodge structures, and further to the level of mixed Hodge modules on the coarse moduli spaces
\Sym^{n}(S)
.

Let
S
be a smooth quasi-projective complex surface. We do not require that
S
is projective, or that there is an isomorphism
K_{S}\cong\mathcal{O}_{S}
, or that
S
is cohomologically pure. We denote by
\mathfrak{M}_{n}(S)
the stack of coherent sheaves on
S
with zero-dimensional support, and length
n
. Denoting by
\varpi_{n}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{n}(S)\to\mathcal{M}_{n}(S)
the good moduli space, there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{M}_{n}(S)\cong\Sym^{n}(S)
. We drop the subscripts and superscripts
n
when considering all possible lengths at once. Let
\Delta_{n}\colon S\rightarrow\Sym^{n}(S)
be the inclusion of the small diagonal. Then one shows as in
[
20
, Appendix A]
that there is an injection of mixed Hodge modules

(\Delta_{n})_{*}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}_{S}\hookrightarrow\tau^{\leqslant 0}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{n}\coloneqq\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{n,\mathrm{Alg}}

where
\underline{\mathscr{A}}=\varpi_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}(S)}
and
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{n}
is the restriction of
\underline{\mathscr{A}}
to
\Sym^{n}(S)
. Furthermore, the BPS algebra is commutative: since perverse sheaves form a stack, this is a local calculation, and follows from the case
S=\mathbf{A}^{2}
. The resulting morphism of algebra objects in
\mathrm{MHM}(\Sym(S))

\Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\bigoplus_{n\geqslant 1}(\Delta_{n})_{*}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}_{S}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{BPS}_{\mathrm{Alg}}^{\mathrm{MHM}}

is an isomorphism of algebra objects: again, this is a local calculation, and is shown in the local case
S=\mathbf{A}^{2}
in
[
20
, Appendix A]
.

The algebra
\underline{\mathscr{A}}
carries the usual
\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}
-action given by the morphism
\det\colon\mathfrak{M}(S)\rightarrow\B\mathbf{C}^{*}
, and so we obtain a morphism of complexes of mixed Hodge modules

\Phi\colon\Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\bigoplus_{n\geqslant 1}(\Delta_{n})_{*}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}_{S}\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}\right)\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}

combining the
\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}
-action with the evaluation morphism given by the relative Hall algebra product.

Proposition 11.1
.

The morphism
\Phi
is an isomorphism of complexes of mixed Hodge modules. Taking derived global sections, there is an isomorphism of mixed Hodge structures

\HO^{*}(\Phi)\colon\Sym\left(\bigoplus_{n\geqslant 1}\HO_{*}^{\BoMo}(S,\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}})\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}\right)\cong\HO_{*}^{\BoMo}(\mathfrak{M}(S),\mathbf{Q}).

The statement regarding
\Phi
is again a local statement, that can be checked by reducing to the case
S=\mathbf{A}^{2}
. Here, it is the original PBW isomorphism of
[
25
]
, using the description of the BPS sheaves found in
[
16
, Section 5]
. The statement regarding
\HO^{*}(\Phi)
at the level of graded vector spaces recovers
[
51
, Thm.7.1.6]
.

11.2.
Semistable coherent sheaves on surfaces

Let
S
be a smooth quasi-projective complex surface, which for now we do not assume has a trivial canonical bundle. Let
H
be an ample line bundle on
\overline{S}
, a projective compactification of
S
. Let
p(t)\in\mathbf{Q}[t]/\sim
be a reduced Hilbert polynomial. We form as in §
6.1
the moduli stack
\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{p(t)}(S)
of compactly supported coherent sheaves which are either the zero sheaf or semistable with normalised Hilbert polynomial
p(t)
, and the coarse moduli space
\mathcal{C}\!oh^{\sst}_{p(t)}(S)
. For simplicity, we assume that
\chi(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})
is even for all coherent sheaves with reduced Hilbert polynomial
p(t)
: this condition can be relaxed, at the cost of dealing with half Tate twists and some sign difficulties. By Proposition
6.13
, the stack
\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{p(t)}(S)
satisfies Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
, and so we may form the relative CoHA

\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{p(t)}(S)\coloneqq\mathtt{JH}_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{p(t)}(S)}^{\mathrm{vir}}\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{C}\!oh^{\sst}_{p(t)}(S))).

This object then carries the
\boxdot
-algebra structure provided in §
9.1
. Note that this structure will involve some Tate twists if
\chi(-,-)
is not symmetric.

Now we assume that
S
satisfies
\mathcal{O}_{S}\cong\omega_{S}
. Then there are no Tate twists appearing in the product, and (see §
6.1
) Assumptions
4
,
5
and
6
are also satisfied, so that (possibly after picking a form
\psi
as in (
1.2
)) we may form the BPS algebra MHM

\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{p(t),\mathrm{Alg}}^{(\psi)}(S)=\tau^{\leqslant 0}\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{(\psi)}_{p(t)}(S).

Finally, we make the assumption that the category of compactly supported semistable coherent sheaves on
S
with reduced Hilbert polynomial is totally negative. Then as special cases of Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
, we deduce the following

Theorem 11.2
.

Let
S
be a smooth quasi-projective complex surface with
\mathcal{O}_{S}\cong\omega_{S}
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be the category of compactly supported semistable coherent sheaves on
S
with reduced Hilbert polynomial
p(t)
. Assume moreover that this category is totally negative. Then the natural morphism

\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\psi)}\colon\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Alg}}\left(\bigoplus_{a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{C}\!oh_{p(t)}^{\sst}(S)_{a})\right)\longrightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{p(t),\mathrm{Alg}}^{(\psi)}(S).

is an isomorphism of
\boxdot
-algebras in
\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{C}\!oh_{p(t)}^{\sst}(S))
.

Theorem 11.3
.

Let
S
be a smooth quasi-projective complex surface satisfying the same conditions as Theorem
11.2
. Define

\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{p(t),\mathrm{Lie}}(S)\coloneqq\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Lie}}\left(\bigoplus_{a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{C}\!oh_{p(t)}^{\sst}(S)_{a})\right).

Then the morphism

\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi}\colon\Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{p(t),\mathrm{Lie}}(S)\otimes\HO^{*}(\B\mathbf{C}^{*},\mathbf{Q})\right)\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\psi}_{p(t)}(S)

is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{C}\!oh^{\sst}_{p(t)}(S)))
(though not of algebra objects). Taking derived global sections, and setting
L\subseteq\mathbf{Q}[t]
to be the monoid of polynomials in the equivalence class
p(t)
, we deduce that there is an isomorphism of
L
-graded mixed Hodge structures

\Sym\left(\operatorname{Free}_{\mathrm{Lie}}\left(\bigoplus_{a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}}\ICA(\mathcal{C}\!oh_{p(t)}^{\sst}(S)_{a})\right)\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}\right)\cong\HO^{\BoMo}_{*}(\mathfrak{Coh}^{\sst}_{p(t)}(S),\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{vir}}).

11.3.
Semistable
B
-modules

Let
B
be an algebra, which we presume is presented in the form
B=A/\langle R\rangle
as in (
6.2
). Recall that we assume that
A
is the universal localisation of the path algebra
\mathbf{C}Q
of a quiver. We fix a stability condition
\zeta\in\mathbf{Q}^{Q_{0}}
. Recall that the
slope
of a
B
-module
M
is defined to be the number

\mu(M)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\dim_{\mathbf{C}}(M)}\dim_{Q_{0}}(M)\cdot\zeta

where
\dim_{Q_{0}}(M)\coloneqq(\dim(e_{i}\cdot M))_{i\in Q_{0}}
. A
B
-module is called
semistable
if for all proper nonzero submodules
M^{\prime}\subseteq M
we have
\mu(M^{\prime})\leqslant\mu(M)
. We denote by
\mathfrak{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{B,\mathbf{d}}
the moduli stack of
\mathbf{d}
-dimensional semistable
B
-modules. There is a good moduli space
\mathfrak{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{B,\mathbf{d}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{B,\mathbf{d}}
constructed by King via GIT
[
44
]
in the special case
B=\mathbf{C}Q
, and then defined in general via base change from this special case. So Assumption
4
is always satisfied.

Fix
\theta\in\mathbf{Q}
. We denote by
\mathfrak{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{B,\theta}
the disjoint union of all
\mathfrak{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{B,\mathbf{d}}
such that
\mathbf{d}\cdot\zeta/\lvert\mathbf{d}\lvert=\theta
, and define
\mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{B,\theta}
to be the union of
\mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{B,\mathbf{d}}
over the same set of dimension vectors. Assumptions
1
-
3
similarly hold for the stack
\mathfrak{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{B,\theta}
via base change from the case
\zeta=(0,\ldots,0)
, i.e. the case in which we consider all
B
-modules to be semistable.

For finiteness of the direct sum map, consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{B,\theta}\times\mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{B,\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{B,\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{B,\theta}\times\mathcal{M}_{B,\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{B,\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\times l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus^{\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}

where
l\colon\mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{B,\theta}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{B,\theta}
is the GIT quotient map, which is projective by construction. Then
\oplus\circ(l\times l)
is projective by Assumption
5
for the category of
B
-modules, and so
\oplus^{\zeta}
is proper, since properness satisfies the 2 out of 3 property. Finally, the category of semistable
B
-modules is a full subcategory of the category of
\widetilde{B}
-modules, so as long as
\widetilde{B}
carries a left 2CY structure, Assumption
6
is satisfied. We summarise in a theorem.

Theorem 11.4
.

Let
B
be an algebra presented as
B=A/\langle R\rangle
where
A
is the localisation of a path algebra of a quiver by a finite set of linear combinations of cyclic paths with the same endpoints, and
R
is a finite set of relations in
A
. Let
\zeta\in\mathbf{Q}^{Q_{0}}
be a stability condition, let
\theta\in\mathbf{Q}
be a slope, and let
\mathtt{JH}\colon\mathfrak{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{\theta}(B)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{\theta}(B)
be the morphism from the stack of
\zeta
-semistable
B
-modules of slope
\theta
to the GIT moduli space. Then

(1)

\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{B,\theta}^{\zeta}\coloneqq\mathtt{JH}_{*}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{\theta}(B)}
carries a (Tate-twisted) algebra structure in
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{\theta}(B)))
.

(2)

Assume moreover that the derived enhancement
\widetilde{B}
is a 2-Calabi–Yau algebra. Then the algebra structure on
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{B,\theta}^{\zeta}
is untwisted, and the subcomplex
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{B,\theta,\mathrm{Alg}}^{\zeta}\coloneqq\tau^{\leqslant 0}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{B,\theta}^{\zeta}
is a
\boxdot
-algebra in
\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta\sst}_{\theta}(B))
.

(3)

Assume finally that the category
\widetilde{B}\lmod
is a totally negative 2CY category. Then there is an isomorphism of
\boxdot
-algebras

\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{B,\theta,\mathrm{Alg}}^{\zeta}\cong\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Alg}}\left(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\theta}^{\zeta}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{\zeta\sst}(B))\right)

where
\Sigma^{\zeta}_{\theta}
is the set of dimension vectors
\mathbf{d}
such that
\mathbf{d}\cdot\zeta/\lvert\mathbf{d}\lvert=\theta
and there exists a
\zeta
-stable
\mathbf{d}
-dimensional
B
-module. In addition, there is a PBW isomorphism of MHM complexes

\Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{B,\theta,\mathrm{Lie}}^{\zeta}\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}\right)\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{B,\theta}^{\zeta}

where

\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{B,\theta,\mathrm{Lie}}^{\zeta}\coloneqq\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot\mathrm{-Lie}}\left(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\theta}^{\zeta}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{d}}^{\zeta\sst}(B))\right).

12.
Freeness of the BPS algebra of totally negative
2
-Calabi–Yau categories

In this section we prove the following theorem, which determines the BPS algebra of a totally negative
2
-Calabi–Yau category.

Theorem 12.1

(= MHM version of Theorem
1.4
)
.

For any totally negative
2
-Calabi–Yau category
\mathcal{A}
satisfying the Assumptions
1
-
6
of §
5
,
the morphism
\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}
is an isomorphism of
\boxdot
-algebras in
\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})
.

Since the functor
\rat\colon\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Perv}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}})
is exact, the mixed Hodge module version implies the perverse sheaf version of the theorem.

Those readers who prefer to think about perverse sheaves rather than mixed Hodge modules may read the proofs in the categories of perverse sheaves/constructible complexes. The key point is that a semisimple mixed Hodge module is sent under
\rat
to a semisimple perverse sheaf
[
23
]
.
3
3

3

However, we issue a warning here that simple mixed Hodge modules are sent to semisimple perverse sheaves that need not be simple.

We prove Theorem
12.1
by reducing to the the case of preprojective algebras of totally negative quivers.

Theorem 12.2

(= MHM version of Theorem
1.2
)
.

For every totally negative quiver
Q
, the morphism
\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}}
(defined in (
10.2
)) is an isomorphism of
\boxdot
-algebras in
\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}})
.

12.1.
Reduction to preprojective algebras of totally negative quivers

12.1.1.
Free algebra of a
\Sigma
-collection

Let
\underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}
be a collection of simple objects in
\mathcal{A}
of classes
a_{i}=[\mathcal{F}_{i}]\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}
and for
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
let
x_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a_{i}}
be the closed
\mathbf{C}
-point corresponding to
\mathcal{F}_{i}
. The inclusions
x_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}
induce a monoid morphism
\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}
sending
1_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
to
x_{i}
.
Let
Q
be half of the Ext-quiver of the collection
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
.
The inclusions
\{S_{i}\}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},e_{i}}
induce a monoid morphism
\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}
sending
1_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
to
S_{i}
.
By Lemma
3.4

\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}}^{!}
and
\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}
are strict monoidal.

For every
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
pick an analytic Ext-quiver neighbourhood

\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{m}}
of the point
x_{\mathbf{m}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a_{\mathbf{m}}}

corresponding to the semisimple object
\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}}
of
class
a_{\mathbf{m}}\coloneqq\sum_{i}m_{i}a_{i}
as in Theorem
5.3
. Set
\mathcal{U}=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{N}^{I}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{m}}
.
As part of an analytic Ext-quiver neighbourhood we have a commutative diagram of analytic spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath}

such that the horizontal morphisms
\jmath,\jmath^{\prime}
are analytic-open embeddings.

Lemma 12.3
.

There is a natural isomorphism of
\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}
-graded mixed Hodge structures

(\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}})^{!}\left(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{m}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{m}})\right)\cong\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\left(\bigoplus_{a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a})\right).

Proof.

Since intersection complexes are stable under pullback along open embeddings, we have isomorphisms

(\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}})^{!}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{m}})\cong y^{!}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{m}})\cong\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a_{\mathbf{m}}}).

Now it suffices to note that

\mathbf{m}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}
if and only if
a_{\mathbf{m}}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}
(Proposition
5.4

(3)
).
∎

Corollary 12.4
.

There is a natural isomorphism of algebras

\gamma_{\operatorname{Free}}\colon(\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}})^{!}\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-{\mathrm{Alg}}}\left(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{m}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{m}})\right)\cong\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-{\mathrm{Alg}}}\left(\bigoplus_{a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a})\right).

Proof.

Since
\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}
and
(\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}})^{!}
are both strict monoidal, they commute with the free algebra construction. The statement now follows from Lemma
12.3
.
∎

12.1.2.
BPS algebra of a
\Sigma
-collection

We keep the notation from §
12.1.1
.

The analytic Ext-quiver neighbourhoods are compatible with good moduli space morphisms in the sense that the diagram in Theorem
5.3
commutes.
Hence the canonical morphisms

(12.1)

\mathcal{H}^{0}\!\left((\jmath_{\Pi_{Q}})^{\ast}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}\right)\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{0}\!\left(p_{\ast}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{U}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\right)\longleftarrow\mathcal{H}^{0}\!\left((\jmath_{\mathcal{A}})^{\ast}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}\right)

are isomorphisms in
\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{U})
, where
p\colon\mathfrak{U}=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbf{m}}\to\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{m}}=\mathcal{U}
is the good moduli space morphism over
\mathcal{U}
.

Since pullback for mixed Hodge modules by an analytic-open embedding is t-exact, the isomorphisms (
12.1
) induce an isomorphism of graded mixed Hodge structures

(12.2)

\gamma_{\aBPS}\colon(\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}})^{!}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}\longrightarrow\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}.

Corollary 12.5
.

The isomorphism
\gamma_{\aBPS}
is an isomorphism of algebras such that the diagram

(12.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}}^{!}\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-{\mathrm{Alg}}}\left(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-{\mathrm{Alg}}}\left(\bigoplus_{a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\operatorname{Free}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}})^{!}\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath^{!}\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\aBPS}}

commutes.

Proof.

By Corollary
9.10
we have the isomorphism of algebras

\gamma\colon(\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}})^{!}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}\to\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Since the multiplication on
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Alg}}
and
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}
is obtained by applying
\tau^{\leqslant 0}
to the multiplication on
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}
(§
10
), respectively,
it follows that
\gamma
restricts to a morphism of algebras
\gamma_{\aBPS}
.
Commutativity follows from the fact that
\gamma_{\aBPS}
restricts to
\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a_{\mathbf{m}}})\cong(\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}})^{!}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}({\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{m}}})
for
\mathbf{m}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}
.
∎

12.1.3.
Proof that Theorem
12.2
implies Theorem
12.1

Lemma 12.6
.

Let
\mathscr{B}\in\mathcal{D}^{+}_{\mathrm{c}}(X)
be a constructible complex on a complex algebraic variety
X
. Then, if
\mathscr{B}\neq 0
, there exists a
\mathbf{C}
-point
i_{x}\colon\mathrm{pt}\rightarrow X
such that
i_{x}^{!}\mathscr{B}\neq 0
.

Proof.

By Verdier duality, it suffices to prove the same result for
i_{x}^{*}
instead of
i_{x}^{!}
. For any
\mathbf{C}
-point
x
of
X
, the functor
i_{x}^{*}
is exact for the natural
t
-structures so that for any
n\in\mathbf{Z}
,
i_{x}^{*}\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mathscr{B})\cong\mathcal{H}^{n}(i_{x}^{*}\mathscr{B})
. Therefore, if
i_{x}^{*}\mathscr{B}=0
for every
x\in X
, the constructible complex
\mathscr{B}
has vanishing cohomology sheaves. By conservativity of the system of cohomology functors (
[
2
, Proposition 1.3.7]
),
\mathscr{B}
itself vanishes.
∎

Corollary 12.7
.

Let
\underline{\mathscr{B}}\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(X))
be a complex of mixed Hodge modules on a complex algebraic variety
X
. Then, if
\underline{\mathscr{B}}\neq 0
, there exists a
\mathbf{C}
-point
i_{x}\colon\mathrm{pt}\rightarrow X
such that
i_{x}^{!}\underline{\mathscr{B}}\neq 0
.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
12.6
to
\rat(\mathscr{B})
.
∎

Lemma 12.8
.

Suppose that
\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}
is not an isomorphism.
Then there is an
x\in\operatorname{supp}(\ker(\Phi_{\mathcal{A}})\oplus\operatorname{coker}(\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}))
with half Ext-quiver and dimension vector
(Q^{\prime},\mathbf{m})
such that the morphism

\imath_{0}^{!}\Phi_{\Pi_{Q^{\prime}},\mathbf{m}}\colon\imath_{0}^{!}\left(\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-{\mathrm{Alg}}}\left(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}})\right)\right)_{\mathbf{m}}\longrightarrow\iota^{!}_{0}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{m}}

is
not
an isomorphism, where
\Phi_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{m}}
is the
\mathbf{m}
th graded piece of
\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}}
and

\imath_{0}\colon\mathrm{pt}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{m}}
is the inclusion of the point corresponding to the trivial
\mathbf{m}
-dimensional representation
S_{\mathbf{m}}
.

Proof.

Write
\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\ker(\Phi_{\mathcal{A}})
and
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\operatorname{coker}(\Phi_{\mathcal{A}})
.
Both
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{C}
are semisimple, because they are subquotients of semisimple mixed Hodge modules (Lemma
10.4
and Proposition
3.5
).
Let
x\in\operatorname{supp}(\mathcal{K}\oplus\mathcal{C})
be such that
\imath_{x}^{!}\mathcal{K}\oplus\imath_{x}^{!}\mathcal{C}\neq 0
.
Let
(Q,\mathbf{m})
be the half Ext-quiver and dimension vector of
x
.
By the commutative diagram (
12.3
) and an analytic Ext-quiver neighbourhood argument as above, we have
\imath_{x}^{!}\mathcal{K}\oplus\imath_{x}^{!}\mathcal{C}=\imath_{0}^{!}\ker(\Phi_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{m}})\oplus\imath_{0}^{!}\operatorname{coker}(\Phi_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{m}})
.
∎

By Proposition
7.1
every half Ext-quiver
Q
of a collection of simples in a totally negative 2CY category must be a totally negative quiver.
Thus, if
\Phi_{\mathcal{A}}
is not an isomorphism, Lemma
12.8
implies that there exists a totally negative quiver
Q
, for which
\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}}
is not an isomorphism, contradicting Theorem
12.2
.
∎

12.2.
The proof for preprojective algebras

We turn to the proof of Theorem
12.2
.

We begin by restating Theorem
12.2
so that we can induct on the (cross-sum of the) dimension vector
\mathbf{d}
and reverse induct on the number of vertices of the quiver
Q
.
The morphism
\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}}\Phi_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
is graded by dimension vector. Thus Theorem
12.2
is equivalent to the following theorem.

Theorem 12.9
.

For every totally negative quiver
Q
and dimension vector
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}
the morphism

\Phi_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}\colon\bigg(\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Alg}}\bigg(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{f}\in\Sigma_{Q}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{f}}(\Pi_{Q}))\bigg)\bigg)_{\mathbf{d}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathtt{JH}_{\mathbf{d},\ast}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}}^{\mathrm{vir}})

is an isomorphism.

Consider the set
\mathrm{QuivDim}
consisting of pairs
(Q,\mathbf{d})
of quivers with dimension vectors supported on the entire quiver.
To formalise the simultaneous induction on the cross-sum of the dimension vectors and the reverse induction on the number of vertices we introduce the function

\begin{split}\mu\colon\mathrm{QuivDim}&\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{>0}\times\mathbf{Z}_{<0}\\
(Q,\mathbf{d})&\longmapsto(\lvert\mathbf{d}\rvert,-\lvert Q_{0}\rvert)=\left(\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}d_{i},-\lvert Q_{0}\rvert\right).\end{split}

The induction will be with respect to the partial order on
\mathrm{QuivDim}
pulled back from the lexicographical order on
\mathbf{Z}_{>0}\times\mathbf{Z}_{<0}
along
\mu
.

12.2.1.
The action of adding a scalar to a loop

Let
Q
be a quiver. Let
L
be the set of loops of
Q
and let
\overline{L}=L\sqcup L^{\ast}
be the set of loops in the doubled quiver
\overline{Q}
. For every loop
l\in L
there are two
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-actions on
\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}
given by adding a scalar multiple of the identity to the loop
l
or to the loop
l^{*}
.
Combining both of the actions for all of the loops, we have a
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
-action on
\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}
which in formulas is given by

(x_{l},x^{*}_{l})_{l\in L}(\rho_{a})_{a\in\overline{Q}_{1}}=\left(\left\{\begin{matrix}\rho_{l}+x_{l}\operatorname{id}_{e_{i}\cdot\rho},&\text{ if $a=l\colon i\to i$ for $l\in L$}\\
\rho_{l^{*}}+x^{*}_{l}\operatorname{id}_{e_{i}\cdot\rho},&\text{ if $a=l^{*}\colon i\to i$ for $l\in L$}\\
\rho_{a}&\text{ otherwise}\end{matrix}\right\}\right)_{a\in\overline{Q}_{1}}

for
(x_{l},x^{*}_{l})_{l\in L}\in\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
and a
\Pi_{Q}
-module
(\rho_{a})_{a\in\overline{Q}_{1}}
.

Lemma 12.10
.

All summands of the pure mixed Hodge modules
\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Alg}}(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}))
and
\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathtt{JH}_{\mathbf{d},\ast}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}}^{\mathrm{vir}})
are
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
-equivariant.

Proof.

The intersection complexes
\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}})
for
\mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}
are
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
-equivariant as they can be defined by intermediate extension of the constant variation of Hodge structure
\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{s}}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{-\dim(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}})/2}
on the
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
-invariant dense open subset
\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}^{s}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
. The monoidal product
\boxdot
is evidently
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
-equivariant. It follows that so is
\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Alg}}(\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\Sigma_{\Pi_{Q}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}))
.

By inspecting the presentation of
\mathtt{JH}
as in §
8.1
one sees that the good moduli space morphism
\mathtt{JH}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}
is equivariant with respect to this
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
-action.
It follows that
\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathtt{JH}_{\mathbf{d},\ast}\mathbb{D}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}}^{\mathrm{vir}})
is
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
-equivariant.
∎

12.2.2.
Comparison of Ext-quivers

To every closed point
x
in the good moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}
we associate a quiver with dimension vector
(Q^{\prime}_{x},\mathbf{m}_{x})\in\mathrm{QuivDim}
given by half of the Ext-quiver and multiplicity vector of a corresponding semisimple
\Pi_{Q}
-module
\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i}^{m_{i}}
.

Lemma 12.11
.

For all
(Q,\mathbf{d})\in\mathrm{QuivDim}
and for all closed points
x\in\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
we have

(1)

\lvert\mathbf{m}_{x}\rvert\leqslant\lvert\mathbf{d}\rvert

(2)

\mu(Q^{\prime}_{x},\mathbf{m}_{x})\leqslant\mu(Q,\mathbf{d})

(3)

The following are equivalent

(a)

\mu(Q_{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{m}_{x})=\mu(Q,\mathbf{d})

(b)

(Q_{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{m}_{x})=(Q,\mathbf{d})

(c)

x
is in the
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
-orbit of (the point corresponding to)
S_{\mathbf{d}}
.

Proof.

For convenience we write
(Q^{\prime},\mathbf{m})
for
(Q^{\prime}_{x},\mathbf{m}_{x})
.
Let
\mathcal{F}=\bigoplus_{j\in Q^{\prime}_{0}}\mathcal{F}_{j}^{\oplus m_{j}}
be the semisimple
\Pi_{Q}
-module of dimension vector
\mathbf{d}
corresponding to
x
(such that all
m_{i}
are nonzero). We have for all
i\in Q_{0}

(12.4)

\sum_{j\in Q^{\prime}_{0}}m_{j}\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}(\mathcal{F}_{j})_{i}=d_{i}.

Summing (
12.4
) over all
i
we have

(12.5)

\sum_{j\in Q^{\prime}_{0}}m_{j}\lvert\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}(\mathcal{F}_{j})\rvert=\lvert\mathbf{d}\rvert.

Part
(i)
of the lemma follows immediately from
\lvert\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}(\mathcal{F}_{j})\rvert\geqslant 1
.
We now prove part
(ii)
.
The case
\lvert\mathbf{m}\rvert>\lvert\mathbf{d}\rvert
, is impossible because it contradicts (
12.5
).
If
\lvert\mathbf{m}\rvert<\lvert\mathbf{d}\rvert
, then by definition of
\mu
we have
\mu(Q^{\prime},\mathbf{m})<\mu(Q,\mathbf{d})
.

On the other hand suppose
\lvert\mathbf{m}\rvert=\lvert\mathbf{d}\rvert
, then we wish to show
\lvert Q^{\prime}_{0}\rvert\geqslant\lvert Q_{0}\rvert
. By (
12.5
) we must have
\lvert\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}{(\mathcal{F}_{j})}\rvert=1
, hence each
\mathcal{F}_{j}
is supported at a single vertex, call it
v(j)
. By (
12.4
) for every
i\in Q_{0}
there is a
w(i)\in Q^{\prime}_{0}
such that
v(w(i))=i
(choose
w(i)
so that
m_{w(i)}\operatorname{\mathbf{dim}}({\mathcal{F}_{w(i)}})_{i}\neq 0
). Thus
w\colon Q_{0}\to Q^{\prime}_{0}
is an injective map with left inverse
v\colon Q^{\prime}_{0}\to Q_{0}
, showing
\lvert Q^{\prime}_{0}\rvert\geqslant\lvert Q_{0}\rvert
.

It remains to characterize the saturation of the inequality. The implications
(c)\Rightarrow(b)\Rightarrow(a)
are clear. Suppose
\mu(Q^{\prime},\mathbf{m})=\mu(Q,\mathbf{d})
. By definition of
\mu
we can identify the vertex sets of the quivers
Q^{\prime}
and
Q
.
As before, by (
12.4
) we deduce that each
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is a 1-dimensional representation supported at the vertex
i
and
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{d}
. Hence
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is the data of a scalar
x_{l}
for every loop
l
in
\overline{Q}
at
i
. Altogether we see that
F
is in the
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
-orbit of
0_{\mathbf{d}}
. This proves part
(iii)
.
∎

12.2.3.
The recursion

The key ingredient for the recursion is the following lemma.

Lemma 12.12
.

Fix a totally negative quiver
Q
and dimension vector
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}
.
Suppose
\Phi_{\Pi_{Q^{\prime}_{x}},\mathbf{m}_{x}}
is an isomorphism for all half Ext quivers with dimension vectors
(Q^{\prime}_{x},\mathbf{m}_{x})\in\mathrm{QuivDim}
for closed points
x\in\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
such that
\mu(Q^{\prime}_{x},\mathbf{m}_{x})<\mu(Q,\mathbf{d})
. Then
\Phi_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Suppose
\Phi_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
is not an isomorphism. The kernel
\mathcal{K}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
and cokernel
\mathcal{C}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
are semisimple mixed Hodge modules on
\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
.
Hence there is a simple direct summand
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
.
We have the following chain of inclusions

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\subseteq\overline{\{x\in\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}\mid\Phi_{\Pi_{x},\mathbf{m}_{x}}\text{ is not an iso.}\}}

(by the proof of Lemma
12.8
)

\displaystyle\subseteq\overline{\{x\in\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}\mid\mu(Q_{x},\mathbf{m}_{x})=\mu(Q,\mathbf{d})\}}

(by hypothesis and Lemma
12.11

(i)
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}\cdot 0_{\mathbf{d}}

(by Lemma
12.11

(iii)
)

In words, the hypothesis guarantees that the support of
\mathcal{T}
is contained in the
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
-orbit
\overline{Z}=\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}\cdot 0_{\mathbf{d}}\cong\mathbf{C}^{\overline{L}}
of
0_{\mathbf{d}}
.
Since
\mathcal{T}
is a
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
-equivariant mixed Hodge module (Lemma
12.10
), its support is
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
-stable and hence equal to the entire orbit
\overline{Z}
.

The groups
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{L}
are contractible, so are any of their orbits. Therefore taking total cohomology induces equivalences

\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}}(\overline{Z})\simeq\mathrm{MHM}(\mathrm{pt})\simeq\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{L}}(Z)

where
Z
is the
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{L}
-orbit of
0_{\mathbf{d}}
. Let
\imath_{\mathcal{SSN}}\colon\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{SSN}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
be the inclusion.
We have a Cartesian square of inclusions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}^{L}\cong Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}^{\mathcal{SSN}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}^{\overline{L}}\cong\overline{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{SSN}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Since
\mathcal{T}
is simple, we have
\mathcal{T}=\underline{\mathcal{IC}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\overline{L}}}\otimes V
for some simple polarizable pure mixed Hodge structure
V
, and
\imath_{\mathcal{SSN}}^{!}\mathcal{T}\cong\underline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{L}}\otimes V
.

Hence
\HO^{0}({\imath_{\mathcal{SSN}}^{!}\mathcal{T}})\cong\rat(V)
is a summand of the kernel or cokernel of
\HO^{0}(\imath_{\mathcal{SSN}}^{!}\Phi_{\Pi_{Q}})
which contradicts Corollary
10.9
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
12.9
.

Fix a quiver with dimension vector
(Q,\mathbf{d})\in\mathrm{QuivDim}
. Without loss of generality we assume that
\mathbf{d}
is supported on all of
Q
.
Consider the following subset of
\mathbf{Z}_{>0}\times\mathbf{Z}_{<0}
.

\displaystyle R(Q,\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\mu(Q^{\prime}_{x},\mathbf{m}_{x})\mid x\in\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}\text{ and }\mu(Q^{\prime}_{x},\mathbf{m}_{x})\neq\mu(Q,\mathbf{d})\}

\displaystyle=\{\mu(Q^{\prime}_{x},\mathbf{m}_{x})\mid x\in\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}\text{ and }\mu(Q^{\prime}_{x},\mathbf{m}_{x})<\mu(Q,\mathbf{d})\}.

\displaystyle(\text{Lemma~\ref{lemma:ExtQuivnotworse}})

In order to apply Lemma
12.12
we need to show that for all
(Q^{\mathrm{new}},\mathbf{d}^{\mathrm{new}})\in\mu^{-1}(R(Q,\mathbf{d}))
the morphism

\Phi_{\Pi_{Q^{\mathrm{new}}},\mathbf{d}^{\mathrm{new}}}
is an isomorphism. We do so inductively.

For all
(Q^{\mathrm{new}},\mathbf{d}^{\mathrm{new}})\in\mu^{-1}(R(Q,\mathbf{d}))
4
4

4

The preimage
E(Q,\mathbf{d})
of
R(Q,\mathbf{d})
under
\mu
is precisely the set of “better” half Ext-quivers with dimension vectors appearing for
(Q,\mathbf{d})
.
we have the strict inclusion of finite sets
R(Q^{\mathrm{new}},\mathbf{d}^{\mathrm{new}})\subsetneqq R(Q,\mathbf{d})
.
By Lemma
12.11

(i)
, the set
R(Q,\mathbf{d})
is bounded hence finite. Thus, after iterating we eventually end at the case where
R(Q^{\mathrm{fin}},\mathbf{d}^{\mathrm{fin}})=\varnothing
, so that the hypothesis in Lemma
12.12
for
(Q^{\mathrm{fin}},\mathbf{d}^{\mathrm{fin}})
is vacuous.

Applying Lemma
12.12
recursively we deduce that
\Phi_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}}
is an isomorphism.
∎

13.
PBW theorem for the CoHA of a totally negative
2
-Calabi–Yau category

In this section, we prove the PBW theorem, Theorem
1.5
.
In the introduction we advocated for the definition of the BPS Lie algebra sheaf of a totally negative 2CY category
\mathcal{A}
as the free Lie algebra object generated by the intersection complexes

\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Lie}}=\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Lie}}\left(\bigoplus_{a\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},a})\right).

To justify the name BPS sheaf it must satisfy the cohomological integrality theorem, meaning that there should be
some
isomorphism as in Theorem
1.5
(not necessarily defined in terms of Hall algebras). In this section we prove Theorem
1.5
for totally negative 2CY categories, that is, we show that the morphism

\Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\mathcal{A},\mathrm{Lie}}\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}}\right)\longrightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\psi}

defined in terms of the relative Hall algebra product of §
9.1
, twisted as in (
10.1
) is an isomorphism.

13.1.
BPS Lie algebras for preprojective algebras of totally negative quivers

An a priori different definition of the BPS Lie algebra sheaf for preprojective algebras of quivers, which we denote by
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathrm{3d},\psi}
, appears in
[
18
, Theorem B]
(see also
[
19
, Theorem/Definition 4.1]
). We will show that our definition of the BPS Lie algebra agrees with this “3d” definition of the BPS Lie algebra.

13.1.1.
PBW theorem for critical CoHAs

We begin by recalling the definition of
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathrm{3d},\psi}
. In
[
25
]
the authors define the BPS mixed Hodge module
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{Q,W}
for the 3-Calabi–Yau category of representations of the Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}(Q,W)
of a symmetric quiver with potential
(Q,W)
. We define

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{Q,W}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\tau^{\leqslant 1}\widetilde{\mathtt{JH}}_{*}\phi_{\Tr(W)}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{Q}}[1]

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{Q,W}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\HO(\mathcal{M}_{Q,W},\tau^{\leqslant 1}\widetilde{\mathtt{JH}}_{*}\phi_{\Tr(W)}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{Q}})

where
\widetilde{\mathtt{JH}}\colon\mathfrak{M}_{Q,W}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{Q,W}
is the usual affinization morphism to the coarse moduli space.
We choose a bilinear form
\psi
on
\mathbf{Z}^{Q_{0}}
satisfying

\displaystyle\psi(a,b)+\psi(b,a)=\langle a,a\rangle\langle b,b\rangle+\langle a,b\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{mod}\;2

for all
a,b\in\mathbf{Z}^{Q_{0}}
.
In
[
49
]
Kontsevich and Soibelman explain how to endow the vanishing cycle cohomology

\mathscr{A}_{Q,W}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}}\HO^{*}(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{d},Q,W},\phi_{\Tr(W)}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{Q}})

with a cohomological Hall algebra structure, defined by pushforward and pullback in vanishing cycle cohomology. We denote by
\mathscr{A}^{\psi}_{Q,W}
the product obtained by multiplying the degree
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})
-piece of the multiplication by the sign
(-1)^{\psi(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})}
. Then we have the following theorem.

Theorem 13.1

(
[
25
]
)
.

The natural morphism
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,W}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{\psi}_{Q,W}
is injective, and its image is moreover closed under the commutator Lie bracket in
\mathscr{A}^{\psi}_{Q,W}
. The morphism

\Sym\left(\mathfrak{g}_{Q,W}\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}\right)\rightarrow\mathscr{A}^{\psi}_{Q,W}

induced by the (
\psi
-twisted) multiplication, and the
\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{*}}
-action on the target, is an isomorphism.

We denote by
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,W}^{\psi}
the resulting Lie algebra; it is called the
BPS Lie algebra
associated to
(Q,W)
. We emphasize that the moduli stack
\mathfrak{M}_{(Q,W)}
of representations of
\operatorname{Jac}(Q,W)
admits a global critical locus description, which makes vanishing cycle techniques especially effective.

13.1.2.
Dimensional reduction

There is a specific choice of quiver with potential that is relevant to the study of
\Pi_{Q}
. We first form the tripled quiver
\widetilde{Q}
by adding a loop
\omega_{i}
to every vertex
i
of the doubled quiver
\overline{Q}
.
Then we consider the canonical cubic potential
\widetilde{W}=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\omega_{i}\sum_{a\in Q_{1}}[a,a^{*}]
, and consider the vanishing cycle mixed Hodge module
\phi_{\Tr(\widetilde{W})}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\widetilde{Q}}}
on the stack of finite-dimensional
\mathbf{C}\widetilde{Q}
-modules, which is supported on
\mathfrak{M}_{(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W})}
.
We define
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}[-1]=\tau^{\leqslant 1}\widetilde{\mathtt{JH}}_{*}\phi_{\Tr(\widetilde{W})}\underline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{\widetilde{Q}}}
as above.

Applying this theory to the tripled quiver with potential a connection to the category of
\Pi_{Q}
-representations is made in
[
16
]
. The category
\operatorname{Jac}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W})
is equivalent to the category of pairs
(M,f)
of a
\Pi_{Q}
-module
M
with an endomorphism
f\colon M\to M
.
Forgetting the endomorphism induces a morphism of stacks
\mathfrak{M}_{(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W})}\to\mathfrak{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}
.
The method of dimensional reduction is used to define
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{3d}}
from
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}

[
16
]
: we define

\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{3d}}=(\mathcal{M}_{(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W})}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}})_{*}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W},\mathrm{Lie}}[-1].

This is a mixed Hodge module (this is an application of the “support lemma” of
[
19
]
), and
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{3d}}
is moreover closed under the commutator Lie bracket in
\underline{\mathscr{A}}^{\psi}_{\Pi_{Q}}
and satisfies the cohomological integrality theorem for
\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}}
via a PBW-type isomorphism (cf. Theorem
13.4
). We denote by
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathrm{3d},\psi}
the resulting
\boxdot
-Lie algebra.

In order to relate the
\psi
-twists occurring in the general theory of §
13.1.1
with the rest of this paper, we use that for all pairs of
\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}
we have

(13.1)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\rangle_{\widetilde{Q}}=(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})_{\Pi_{Q}}

\displaystyle\quad\textrm{mod }2.

It follows that
\psi(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})\coloneqq\langle\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\rangle_{Q}
is a valid choice for the bilinear form
\psi
.

For the comparison of
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}
with
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathrm{3d},\psi}
we use the following theorem that relates the BPS Lie algebra to the BPS algebra for preprojective algebras of quivers.

Theorem 13.2

(
[
19
, Theorem 6.1]
)
.

Let
Q
be a quiver. There is a canonical inclusion
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathrm{3d},\psi}\hookrightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi}
of
\boxdot
-Lie algebras which induces an isomorphism of
\boxdot
-algebras
\UEA(\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathrm{3d},\psi})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi}
.

Suppose now that
Q
is a totally negative quiver. By similar arguments as in the proof of Lemma
10.5
, there exist morphisms

\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathbf{d}})\to\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi}\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi}
which
factor through the inclusion
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathrm{3d},\psi}\hookrightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi}
(see
[
19
, §7.1.1]
for details). Thus, by the universal property of free Lie algebras, they induce a morphism of Lie algebras

\hat{\Phi}_{\Pi_{Q}}\colon\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}\longrightarrow\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathrm{3d},\psi}.

By composing universal properties, the universal enveloping algebra of a free Lie algebra is always the free
algebra on the same generator(s). Altogether we have the following commutative diagram.

(13.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Lie}}(\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\Sigma}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathrm{3d},\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Alg}}(\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\Sigma}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}^{\psi}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\Phi}^{\psi}_{\Pi_{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\psi}_{\Pi_{Q}}}

Corollary 13.3
.

For all totally negative quivers
Q
the morphism
\hat{\Phi}^{\psi}_{\Pi_{Q}}
of
\boxdot
-Lie algebras is an isomorphism.

Proof.

By the PBW theorem for Lie algebra objects in symmetric tensor categories we
have the equations in
\K(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}))

\displaystyle\left[\Sym_{\boxdot}(\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Lie}}(\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\Sigma})))\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Alg}}(\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\Sigma}))\right]

\displaystyle(\UEA(\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Lie}})=\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-{\mathrm{Alg}}})

\displaystyle=\left[\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Alg}}\right]

\displaystyle(\text{Theorem~\ref{theorem:FreeAlgPreProj}})

\displaystyle=\left[\Sym_{\boxdot}(\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathrm{3d}})\right]

\displaystyle(\text{Theorem~\ref{theorem:envBPSLieisBPSalg} = \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{davison2020bps}{}{}, Theorem~6.1]}}).

It follows that in
\K(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}))
we have
\left[\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Lie}}(\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q},\Sigma}))\right]=\left[\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{3d}}\right]
.

Since
\Phi^{\psi}_{\Pi_{Q}}
is a monomorphism (by Theorem
12.2
)
and the diagram (
13.2
) is commutative, it follows that
\hat{\Phi}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi}
is also a monomorphism.
Thus
\hat{\Phi}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi}
is a monomorphism between two semisimple mixed Hodge modules of the same class in
\K(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}))
, hence
it must be an isomorphism.
∎

13.2.
PBW and Cohomological integrality for totally negative 2CY categories

We recall the following theorem:

Theorem 13.4

(
[
16
]
)
.

Let
Q
be a quiver. The morphism
\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}}\to\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi}
induced by the
\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}}
-action on the target, along with the multiplication on the target, induces an isomorphism in
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}))

\widetilde{\Phi}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi}\colon\Sym_{\boxdot}(\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathrm{3d},\psi}\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi}.

We can restrict this isomorphism to the submonoid
\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}
.

Corollary 13.5
.

We have a PBW isomorphism

\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}}^{!}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi}\colon\Sym_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Alg}}\left(\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}}^{!}(\underline{\mathcal{BPS}}_{\Pi_{Q},\mathrm{Lie}}^{\mathrm{3d},\psi})\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}}\right)\rightarrow\imath_{\mathrm{Nil}}^{!}(\mathscr{A}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\psi})=\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\Pi_{Q}}^{\mathrm{nil},\psi},

in the symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathbf{N}^{\underline{Q_{0}}})
.

Proof.

The functor
\imath^{!}\colon\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\Pi_{Q}}))\to\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}))
is a strict monoidal functor by Lemma
3.4
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

We want to prove that the morphism of of objects in
\mathcal{D}^{+}(\mathrm{MHM}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}))

\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi}\colon\Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Lie}}(\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},\Sigma}))\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}}\right)\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}}^{\psi}

defined via the Hall algebra product on the target is an isomorphism.

Let
\mathrm{cone}(\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi})
be the cone of
\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi}
, so that we have a distinguished triangle

\Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Lie}}(\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},\Sigma}))\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}}\right)\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi}}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}}\rightarrow\mathrm{cone}(\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi})\rightarrow.

If
i_{S}\colon S\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
is a subspace, we have a distinguished triangle

i_{S}^{!}\Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Lie}}(\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},\Sigma}))\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}}\right)\xrightarrow{i_{S}^{!}\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi}}i_{S}^{!}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}}\rightarrow i_{S}^{!}\mathrm{cone}(\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi})\rightarrow.

The set
S
will later be specialised to the closed discrete submonoid of
\mathcal{M}
corresponding to a
\Sigma
-collection.

If, for a contradiction,
\mathrm{cone}(\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi})
does not vanish, there exists a closed
\mathbf{C}
-point
x\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}
such that, if
i_{x}
is the inclusion of
x
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}
,
i_{x}^{!}\mathrm{cone}(\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi})\neq 0
(by Corollary
12.7
). Let
\underline{\mathcal{F}}=\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{r}\}
be the
\Sigma
-collection of simple objects of
\mathcal{A}
associated to
x
. We let
Q
be a half of the Ext-quiver of
\underline{\mathcal{F}}
. The morphism of monoids
\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\colon\mathbf{N}^{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}
of §
5.4.5
induces a distinguished triangle

\Sym_{\boxdot}\left(\operatorname{Free}_{\boxdot-\mathrm{Lie}}(\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\underline{\mathcal{IC}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},\Sigma}))\otimes\HO_{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}}\right)\xrightarrow{\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi}}\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}(\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathtt{JH}})\rightarrow\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\mathrm{cone}(\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi})\rightarrow.

By the compatibility of the CoHA multiplication on fibres (Corollary
9.10
), and by the PBW theorem for preprojective algebras of quivers (more specifically by Corollary
13.5
and Corollary
13.3
),
\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi}
is an isomorphism. Therefore,
\imath_{\underline{\mathcal{F}}}^{!}\mathrm{cone}(\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi})
vanishes and so does
i_{x}^{!}\mathrm{cone}(\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi})
. We obtain a contradiction, proving that
\widetilde{\Phi}^{\psi}
is an isomorphism.
∎

13.3.
